Testing conditional independence under isotonicity
Rohan Hore, Jake A. Soloff, Rina Foygel Barber, Richard J. Samworth

Abstract
We propose a test of the conditional independence of random variables X X and Y Y given Z Z under the additional assumption that X X is stochastically nondecreasing in Z Z . The well-documented hardness of testing conditional independence means that some further restriction on the null hypothesis parameter space is required. In contrast to existing approaches based on parametric models, smoothness assumptions, or approximations to the conditional distribution of X X given Z Z and/or Y Y given Z Z , our test requires only the stochastic monotonicity assumption. Our procedure, called PairSwap-ICI , determines the significance of a statistic by randomly swapping the X X values within ordered pairs of Z Z values. The matched pairs and the test statistic may depend on both Y Y and Z Z , providing the analyst with significant flexibility in constructing a powerful test. Our test offers finite-sample Type I error control, and provably achieves high power against a large class of alternatives. We validate our theoretical findings through a series of simulations and real data experiments.



1
Introduction

Testing conditional independence (CI) has emerged in recent years as a core contemporary statistical challenge for practitioners and theoreticians alike. On the practical side, it underpins the problems of variable selection, graphical modelling and causal inference, while from a
theoretical perspective, it has recently been understood that there is a strong sense in which the problem is fundamentally hard without further restrictions, and this has led to great interest in reasonable additional conditions under which one can establish non-trivial CI tests.

To formalize our setting, consider the CI null hypothesis

H_{0}^{\textnormal{CI}}:X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z,

where
X
and
Y
are variables of interest (such as a treatment
X
and an outcome
Y
), while
Z
represents a confounder. Our available data consist of independent copies
(X_{1},Y_{1},Z_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n},Z_{n})
of
(X,Y,Z)\sim P
, where
P
is an unknown distribution on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
. Throughout this paper, we write
P_{X\mid Z}
and
P_{Y\mid Z}
to denote the conditional distributions of
X
given
Z
and of
Y
given
Z
respectively.

Given the clear practical relevance of conditional independence testing, as well as the well-established literature on unconditional independence testing, the following hardness result due to
Shah and Peters (2020)
initially comes as a big surprise.

Theorem 1

(
Shah and Peters, 2020
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{Cont}}
denote the set of distributions of
(X,Y,Z)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, any test of the null
H_{0}^{\textnormal{CI}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{Cont}}
with Type I error level
\alpha
has power no greater than
\alpha
at every alternative distribution in
\mathcal{P}_{\textnormal{Cont}}\setminus H_{0}^{\textnormal{CI}}
.

In other words, any valid test of the conditional independence null is powerless at
any
alternative distribution.
We also refer the reader to
Neykov et al. (2021)
and
Kim et al. (2022b)
for further discussion and results on the fundamental hardness of constructing distribution-free tests for CI. Naturally, these hardness results have led to growing interest in identifying natural and minimal assumptions under which non-trivial CI tests can be developed. Existing approaches typically aim to guarantee validity (Type I error control) over restricted classes of null distributions that impose one of the following additional structures:

(a)

a parametric model, such as joint Gaussianity of
(X,Y,Z)
, or a Gaussian linear model for
Y\mid(X,Z)

(
Kalisch and Bühlmann, 2007
;
Epskamp et al., 2018
;
Colombo et al., 2012
, e.g.,)
;

(b)

a known or well-estimated conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}

(
Candès et al., 2018
;
Barber et al., 2020
;
Berrett et al., 2020
;
Niu et al., 2024
)
; or

(c)

smoothness of the conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}

(
Shah and Peters, 2020
;
Lundborg et al., 2022
;
Kim et al., 2022b
;
Lundborg et al., 2024
)
, e.g. it belongs to a Hölder class.

A common limitation of these approaches is that these structural assumptions require specifying information about the underlying model or data distribution. In contrast, we will consider a different setting, where we restrict the null by placing a shape constraint on the conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}
(or alternatively
P_{Y\mid Z}
), which requires neither estimating any distributions nor selecting any tuning parameter, and is typically motivated by the application.

1.1
Our approach

In this work, we introduce a nonparametric structure under which we can test conditional independence: we assume a shape constraint—specifically, a form of stochastic monotonicity—for the conditional distribution of
X\mid Z
. A common application of conditional independence testing is to determine whether one or more covariates are needed in a regression model for a response after accounting for confounders
Z
. In our context, and by analogy with the ‘Model-
X
’ framework of
Candès et al. (2018)
, it is convenient to regard
X
as our response and
Y
as the covariates of interest. Typically in such practical applications,
X
is univariate, and our initial exposition will therefore focus on this case, while allowing
Y
and
Z
to take values in general measurable spaces
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
(the space
\mathcal{Z}
should be equipped with a partial order). Later, in Section
5
, we demonstrate how our framework, methodology and theory can be extended to accommodate multivariate
X
.

Our stochastic monotonicity assumption can be formulated as follows:

Assumption 1

(Monotonicity of the conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
, and
\preceq
be a partial order on
\mathcal{Z}
. We assume
X
is stochastically nondecreasing in
Z
, meaning that

\textnormal{if $z\preceq z^{\prime}$ then }x\mathbb{P}_{{P}}\left\{{X\geq x}\ \middle|\ {Z=z}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}_{{P}}\left\{{X\geq x}\ \middle|\ {Z=z^{\prime}}\right\}\textnormal{ for all $x$}.

Assumption
1
is motivated by several applications, particularly in biomedicine, where, for instance, factors such as smoking intensity may be associated with increased risk of certain diseases or conditions. We observe similar trends in economics, where a greater education level is often linked to higher salaries, and in credit risk modelling, where better credit scores are associated with higher chances of loan approval.

This assumption does not fall into any of our categories (a)–(c), which summarize existing literature on conditional independence tests, and as illustrated by the examples above, may be naturally motivated by applications. To the best of our knowledge, there are no existing non-trivial CI tests under such nonparametric shape constraints.

In Section
2
, we establish that our proposed framework provides a valid CI test for any space
\mathcal{Z}
equipped with a partial ordering under which Assumption
1
holds. For example, when
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{Z}}
, a common choice is the coordinate-wise order, which is appropriate for instance in settings where increases in any of the risk factors such as age, smoking intensity or exposure to air pollution are associated with higher chances of developing certain diseases.

Similar to the general framework of (a)–(c), we address the hardness of conditional independence testing via a restricted null hypothesis, namely the isotonic conditional independence (ICI) null,

\displaystyle H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}:X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z,\textnormal{ and $P_{X\mid Z}$ satisfies Assumption\penalty\ \ref{asm:st}.}

(1)

Naturally, this test should only be applied in settings where the monotonicity condition of Assumption
1
is well-motivated, so that a rejection of
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
can reasonably be interpreted as evidence that
X\not\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z
. However, we emphasise again that some additional assumption beyond
H_{0}^{\textnormal{CI}}
is essential for any valid test to have non-trivial power at any alternative.

The idea behind our framework is to consider carefully-chosen pairs of data points
(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})
and
(X_{j},Y_{j},Z_{j})
with
Z_{i}\preceq Z_{j}
. Under
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, we expect
X_{i}\leq X_{j}
more often than not, and a substantial violation of this provides evidence of the influence of
Y
, i.e. evidence against
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. To calibrate the test appropriately under the null, we employ a particular type of permutation test, where permutations are restricted within matched pairs. To set the stage for the notation and ideas underlying our methodology, we briefly review the simpler framework of testing the null hypothesis of marginal independence,
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y
, and illustrate how permutations facilitate a valid test in that setting.

1.2
Background: testing independence

Given a joint distribution
P
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P
, and write
\mathbf{X}=(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
\mathbf{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
. We can reframe the problem of testing marginal independence as testing whether the entries of
\mathbf{X}
are independent and identically distributed given
\mathbf{Y}
. Specifically,
permutation tests look for violations of exchangeability of
\mathbf{X}
given
\mathbf{Y}
. The general approach proceeds as follows: based on
\mathbf{Y}
, the analyst chooses any statistic
T:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, with larger values of
T(\mathbf{X})
indicating evidence against the independence null.
1
1

1

We emphasise that
T(\mathbf{X})
is allowed to depend on both
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
—for instance we may define the function as
T(\mathbf{x})=|\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}Y_{i}|
, though we suppress the dependence on
\mathbf{Y}
in our notation.
Write
\mathcal{S}_{n}
for the set of permutations of
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and for
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, let
T_{\sigma}=T(\mathbf{X}^{\sigma})
denote the value of the statistic when the entries of
\mathbf{X}
are permuted according to
\sigma
—that is,
\mathbf{X}^{\sigma}=(X_{\sigma(1)},\dots,X_{\sigma(n)})
. Finally, define a
p
-value

p=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T_{\sigma}\geq T}\right\}.

This construction produces a valid
p
-value for
any
choice of test statistic
T
, and the statistic
T
can be tailored to have power against certain specific alternatives. Indeed, this strategy has been successfully employed to construct independence tests via nearest neighbor distances and mutual information
(
Berrett and Samworth, 2019
)
, moment methods
(
Kim et al., 2022a
)
, kernels
(
Pfister et al., 2018
)
, the Hilbert–Schmidt independence criterion
(
Albert et al., 2022
)
and
U
-statistics
(
Berrett and Samworth, 2021
;
Berrett et al., 2021
)
.

In our work, since we are interested in conditional (rather than marginal) independence, we will follow a similar strategy, but will use a restricted class of functions
T
and a (data-dependent) subgroup of permutations
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, both of which respect the stochastic monotonicity condition specified in Assumption
1
.

1.3
Our contributions

In Section
2
, we introduce our general methodology for testing
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure. Our framework allows practitioners flexibility in specifying a matching, i.e., a sequence of ordered pairs of observations, and a test statistic for each matched pair; these latter quantities are then aggregated across all matched pairs to form the overall test statistic. We calibrate the test by comparing the observed statistic to its counterpart computed on
X
values permuted within matched pairs, and prove that the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure guarantees finite-sample Type I error control over the null
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, regardless of the matching or test statistic chosen.

Notwithstanding our general validity guarantee, the matching and test statistic for the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure must be chosen carefully in order to achieve good power against alternatives of interest. Accordingly, in Section
3
, we provide specific recommendations for these choices, motivated both by consideration of oracle strategies for maximizing power and by heuristics for how these can be approximated in practice.
This intuition is then formalized in Section
4
, where in Theorem
5
we provide an asymptotic expression for the power of a general version of the
PairSwap-ICI
test under a broad family of regression models for
X
conditional on
(Y,Z)
. We further introduce a quantity called the isotonic signal-to-noise ratio (
\mathrm{ISNR}
), and show that it controls the power of any valid test of
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. These results turn out to be closely connected, because we prove in Theorems
8
and
9
that the empirical analog of
\mathrm{ISNR}
characterizes the power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test under both oracle and plug-in matching schemes and under the additional assumption that
d_{Z}=1
and
\mathcal{Z}
is equipped with the usual total order on
\mathbb{R}
.

After extending our methodology and theory to multivariate
X
in Section
5
, our simulations in Section
6
validate our theory, confirming that our test is not excessively conservative and has power to detect departures from
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
provided that the signal-to-noise ratio exceeds the critical rate determined by our theory. As an illustration of an application of the
PairSwap-ICI
test on real data, in Section
7
we consider a diabetes dataset from the Pima community near Phoenix, Arizona. One goal here is to assess which of six variables are individual risk factors for diabetes, after controlling for age. Finally, we conclude with a discussion in Section
8
. Most proofs are deferred to the Appendix.

2
Methodology

In this section, we take
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
to be measurable spaces with
\mathcal{Z}
having a partial order, and let
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
. Here we present our general procedure, called the
PairSwap-ICI
test, for testing the isotonic conditional independence null
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. This builds on the intuition outlined in the introduction, whereby pairs of data points
(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})
and
(X_{j},Y_{j},Z_{j})
with large positive differences
X_{i}-X_{j}
and
Z_{i}\preceq Z_{j}
provide evidence against the null.

More formally, based on
\mathbf{Y}=(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
\mathbf{Z}=(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, and without access to
\mathbf{X}=(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, the analyst chooses:

(i)

A sequence of ordered pairs

(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{L},j_{L})

of indices in
[n]
, where all
2L
entries are distinct.
We require the pairs to be ordered in the sense that

\displaystyle Z_{i_{\ell}}\preceq Z_{j_{\ell}}

(2)

for each
\ell\in[L]
. Such a choice of ordered pairs (with any
L\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
) is referred to as a
matching
, and we denote the set of all possible matchings in
[n]
satisfying (
2
) as
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
. In Section
3
, we show how matched pairs can be carefully selected to encode information about
\mathbf{Y}
and thereby enhance the power to detect specific conditional dependencies.

(ii)

A sequence of functions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{L}
, where each
\psi_{\ell}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the following
anti-monotonicity
property:

Definition 1
.

A function
\psi:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to satisfy anti-monotonicity if

\displaystyle\psi(x+\Delta,x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime})-\psi(x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime},x+\Delta)\geq\psi(x,x^{\prime})-\psi(x^{\prime},x)

(3)

for all
x,x^{\prime}
and all
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
.

An example of a class of functions
\psi
that satisfy anti-monotonicity is
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=f(x-x^{\prime})
for any monotone nondecreasing function
f
that is also
odd
in the sense that
f(x)=-f(-x)
, for instance,
\psi_{\ell}(x,x^{\prime})=w_{\ell}\cdot(x-x^{\prime})
or
\psi_{\ell}(x,x^{\prime})=w_{\ell}\cdot\textnormal{sign}(x-x^{\prime})
. As with the choice of matching, in Section
3
, we show how the functions
{\psi_{\ell}}
can be selected advantageously; for instance, when
\psi_{\ell}(x,x^{\prime})=w_{\ell}\cdot(x-x^{\prime})
, dependence on
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
is simply captured through the weights
w_{\ell}
.

With these choices in place, our test statistic
T:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as
2
2

2

Again, as for marginal permutation tests in Section
1.2
, here we suppress dependence on
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}
in the notation
T(\mathbf{x})
, even though this statistic does depend on
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}
through the choices of the matched pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
and functions
\psi_{\ell}
.

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\psi_{\ell}(x_{i_{\ell}},x_{j_{\ell}}).

(4)

In order to calibrate the test, the analyst compares the observed test statistic
T=T(\mathbf{X})
with versions of
T
where indices within pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
are randomly swapped. Specifically, for
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{L})\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}
, define
T_{\mathbf{s}}=T(\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}})
, where
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}}
is a swapped version of the data vector
\mathbf{X}
, with entries

\begin{cases}(X_{i_{\ell}}^{\mathbf{s}},X_{j_{\ell}}^{\mathbf{s}})=(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})&s_{\ell}=+1,\\
(X_{i_{\ell}}^{\mathbf{s}},X_{j_{\ell}}^{\mathbf{s}})=(X_{j_{\ell}},X_{i_{\ell}})&s_{\ell}=-1.\end{cases}

Thus,
s_{\ell}=-1
indicates that the random variables
X_{i_{\ell}}
and
X_{j_{\ell}}
are swapped in
(X_{i_{\ell}}^{\mathbf{s}},X_{j_{\ell}}^{\mathbf{s}})
compared with
(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})
, while
s_{\ell}=+1
indicates no swap.
Informally, the two constraints (
2
) and (
3
) ensure that, under the null, each
\psi_{\ell}(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})
is likely to be no larger than its swapped version,
\psi_{\ell}(X_{j_{\ell}},X_{i_{\ell}})
—and thus, the statistic
T=T(\mathbf{X})
is likely to be no larger than its swapped copies,
T_{\mathbf{s}}=T(\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}})
. If instead
T>T_{\mathbf{s}}
for many swaps
\mathbf{s}
, this indicates evidence against the null. Indeed, we may define

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T_{\mathbf{s}}\geq T}\right\},

(5)

and in Theorem
2
we show that this is a valid
p
-value. In words, this
p
-value compares the observed value
T
of the statistic against all possible permuted statistic values
T_{\mathbf{s}}
that we would obtain by swapping indices within matched pairs of our observed data
\mathbf{X}
.

Example: a linear test statistic.

Before proceeding, we give a simple example of a test statistic that we might choose to use: consider a linear test statistic,

T(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}x_{i}

for some coefficients
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
(which may depend on
\mathbf{Y}
,
\mathbf{Z})
. This function can be used as the test statistic for the
PairSwap-ICI
test, as long as the coefficients satisfy

\beta_{i_{\ell}}\geq\beta_{j_{\ell}}\textnormal{ for each $\ell\in[L]$}.

(6)

To see why, first note that without loss of generality we can take
\beta_{i}=0
for all
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{1},j_{1},\dots,i_{L},j_{L}\}
, i.e., all data points not belonging to any of the
L
pairs. This is because the indicator
{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T_{\mathbf{s}}\geq T}\right\}
, appearing in the computation of the
p
-value, is invariant to these terms. Next,
define

\psi_{\ell}(x,x^{\prime})=\beta_{i_{\ell}}x+\beta_{j_{\ell}}x^{\prime},

which satisfies (
3
) because for any
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
with
x,x^{\prime},x+\Delta,x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\psi_{\ell}(x+\Delta,x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime})-\psi_{\ell}(x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime},x+\Delta)-\psi_{\ell}(x,x^{\prime})+\psi_{\ell}(x^{\prime},x)=(\beta_{i_{\ell}}-\beta_{j_{\ell}})(\Delta+\Delta^{\prime})\geq 0

by our assumption (
6
). We then have
T(\mathbf{x})
equal to the test statistic defined in (
4
).

We remark that choosing such a test statistic is by no means implying an assumption that the dependence between
X
and
Y
given
Z
follows a linear model
—it may be the case that the statistic
T(\mathbf{x})=\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}
has good power for distinguishing the null from the alternative even if a linear model is only a coarse approximation to the true model.

2.1
Validity

Our first main result establishes that our method yields a valid test of
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
.

Theorem 2
.

Under
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, the conditional Type I error of the
PairSwap-ICI
test satisfies
\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\alpha
for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
. In particular, the test enjoys marginal error control:
\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\right\}\leq\alpha
for all
\alpha
.

Proof.

Our proof is split into three steps. First we derive some deterministic properties of the
p
-value. Next, we compare to the
sharp null
, where the pair
X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}}
are identically distributed (rather than stochastically ordered). Finally, we examine the validity of the test under the sharp null.

Step 1: some deterministic properties of the
p
-value.

First, fix
\alpha\in[0,1]
and define a function
p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
as

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})\geq T(\mathbf{x})}\right\},

(7)

so that the
p
-value in (
5
) can be written as

p=p(\mathbf{X})
. For each
\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}
, we can observe that the value
p(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})
is simply computing the quantile of
T(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})
among all possible swapped statistics,
\big(T(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}):\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}\big)
. Consequently, it holds deterministically that

\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})\leq\alpha}\right\}\leq\alpha.

(8)

(Lemma
D.1
in the Appendix verifies this bound, for completeness.)

In addition, we claim that
p(\cdot)
is monotone in its coordinates, namely,
p(\mathbf{x})
is nonincreasing in each
x_{i_{\ell}}
, and nondecreasing in each
x_{j_{\ell}}
. To see why, for each
\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})\geq T(\mathbf{x})}\right\}

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\sum_{\ell:s_{\ell}=+1}\psi_{\ell}(x_{i_{\ell}},x_{j_{\ell}})+\sum_{\ell:s_{\ell}=-1}\psi_{\ell}(x_{j_{\ell}},x_{i_{\ell}})\geq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\psi_{\ell}(x_{i_{\ell}},x_{j_{\ell}})}\right\}

\displaystyle={\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\sum_{\ell:s_{\ell}=-1}\bigl(\psi_{\ell}(x_{i_{\ell}},x_{j_{\ell}})-\psi_{\ell}(x_{j_{\ell}},x_{i_{\ell}})\bigr)\leq 0}\right\}.

By the anti-monotonicity condition (
3
) on
\psi_{\ell}
, this function is nonincreasing in each
x_{i_{\ell}}
, and nondecreasing in each
x_{j_{\ell}}
, and therefore the same is true for
p(\mathbf{x})
as well.

Step 2: compare to the sharp null.

For each
i\in[n]
, let
P_{i}=P_{X|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{i})
denote the null distribution of
X_{i}
(after conditioning on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
).
By Assumption
1
, we know that
P_{i_{\ell}}\preceq_{\rm st}P_{j_{\ell}}
for each pair
\ell\in[L]
, where
\preceq_{\rm st}
denotes the stochastic ordering on distributions. Next, we also define distributions
\bar{P}_{\ell}
for each
\ell\in[L]
, given by the mixture

\bar{P}_{\ell}=\frac{1}{2}P_{i_{\ell}}+\frac{1}{2}P_{j_{\ell}}.

In particular, then, conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
,

P_{i_{\ell}}\preceq_{\rm st}\bar{P}_{\ell}\preceq_{\rm st}P_{j_{\ell}},\quad\ell\in[L].

(9)

We will now compare the observed data values, whose distribution (conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
) is given by

\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\ \sim\ P_{1}\times\dots\times P_{n},

against a different distribution,

\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}=\bigl((X_{\sharp})_{1},\dots,(X_{\sharp})_{n}\bigr)\ \sim\ (P_{\sharp})_{1}\times\dots\times(P_{\sharp})_{n},

where the distributions
(P_{\sharp})_{i}
are defined by setting

(P_{\sharp})_{i_{\ell}}=(P_{\sharp})_{j_{\ell}}=\bar{P}_{\ell}

for each
\ell\in[L]
(and, for any index
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{1},j_{1},\dots,i_{L},j_{L}\}
that does not belong to any of the
L
matched pairs, we simply take
(P_{\sharp})_{i}=P_{i}
).
We can think of this alternative vector of observations as being drawn from a
sharp null
, because for each pair
\ell
, the random variables
(X_{\sharp})_{i_{\ell}},(X_{\sharp})_{j_{\ell}}
are identically distributed (rather than stochastically ordered, as for
X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}}
). In particular, this implies that for any
\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}
,

(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}

(10)

after conditioning on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
.

In Step 1, we verified that the function
p(\mathbf{x})
is nonincreasing in each
x_{i_{\ell}}
, and nondecreasing in each
x_{j_{\ell}}
. In particular, combined with the stochastic ordering (
9
), this means that

p(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})\preceq_{\rm st}p(\mathbf{X})

(conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
). We therefore have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X})\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

From this point on, then, we only need to verify the validity of the
p
-value
p(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})
computed under the sharp null.

Step 3: validity under the sharp null.

For data
\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}
drawn under a sharp null, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}}\bigr)\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\\
=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}}\bigr)\leq\alpha}\right\}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]\leq\alpha,

where the first step holds by (
10
), while the last step holds by the deterministic calculation (
8
) from Step 1.
∎

The
p
-value constructed in (
5
) requires computing
T_{\mathbf{s}}=T(\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}})
for all
2^{L}
values of
\mathbf{s}\in\{-1,1\}^{L}
, which may be computationally prohibitive for moderate or large
L
. In practice, it is common to use a Monte Carlo approximation to the
p
-value: we sample
\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textnormal{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{L})
, and then compute

\hat{p}_{M}=\frac{1+\sum_{m=1}^{M}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T_{\mathbf{s}^{(m)}}\geq T}\right\}}{1+M}.

The extra ‘
1+
’ term appearing in the numerator and denominator is necessary to ensure error control for this Monte Carlo version of our test
(
Davison and Hinkley, 1997
;
Phipson and Smyth, 2010
)
; in particular, this correction ensures we cannot have
\hat{p}_{M}=0
. The following theorem verifies that this version of the test also controls the Type I error.

Theorem 3
.

Fix any
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Under
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\alpha
for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
, and consequently,
\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\leq\alpha}\right\}\leq\alpha
.

We emphasise that the Type I error control of the
PairSwap-ICI
method holds regardless of the dimensions of
\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
; in fact, these may even be infinite-dimensional. In Section
5
we show that the same validity guarantee holds under our extended framework that permits
X
to be multivariate.

3
Designing a powerful
PairSwap-ICI
test

Our
PairSwap-ICI
methodology from Section
2
offers great flexibility to the analyst, who may select any pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
subject to the ordering constraint (
2
), and any functions
\psi_{\ell}
satisfying anti-monotonicity (
3
). In particular, they can decide on these aspects of the test
after
exploring the data
\textbf{Y},\textbf{Z}
, choosing to include a pair
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
if the data observed in
\mathbf{Y}
indicates that
X_{i_{\ell}}>X_{j_{\ell}}
would be likely under the alternative of interest. However, the quality of the matches
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
and functions
\psi_{\ell}
affects the power of our test. The aim of this section, then, is to construct a principled, powerful implementation of our test, by designing concrete choices for the pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
and the functions
\psi_{\ell}
introduced in Section
2
. Throughout, we will restrict our attention to test statistics
T(\mathbf{x})
of the form

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}\,\psi(x_{i_{\ell}},x_{j_{\ell}})

(11)

for some
w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}\geq 0
. That is, in the original definition of the test statistic (
4
), we take
\psi_{\ell}(\cdot)=w_{\ell}\psi(\cdot)
for some vector of non-negative weights
\mathbf{w}=(w_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}
and some
fixed
kernel
\psi
, which is required to satisfy the anti-monotonicity property (
3
).

With this simplification, designing a test statistic now requires specifying the kernel
\psi
, deciding which pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
are matched, and finally, how much weight
w_{\ell}
to assign to each pair, as depicted in this flowchart:

Kernel
\psi
Matching
M=\{(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{L}
Weights
\mathbf{w}=(w_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}

As guaranteed by Theorem
2
, our test controls the Type I error for any choice of
\psi
satisfying (
3
),
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
, and non-negative weight vector
\mathbf{w}
. However, for the test to be effective, we need to tailor these choices to the specific application of interest.
Of course, all of these choices interact with each other: what constitutes a good matching depends on how we choose the weights, and vice versa.

3.1
Specifying the kernel
\psi

We begin by considering several simple options for the kernel
\psi
. As a first example, consider
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
. This choice of
\psi
means that
\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})
is likely to be non-positive under the null (since
Z_{i_{\ell}}\preceq Z_{j_{\ell}}
), but under the alternative, may be likely to be positive and large (if the pair
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
is chosen wisely). Alternatively, we may consider nonlinear test statistics to handle a broader range of settings. If
X
has heavy tails, then the distribution of a linear statistic
T
can be very sensitive to extreme values. We can ameliorate this sensitivity by using
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=\textnormal{sign}(x-x^{\prime})
, or
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=(-K)\vee(x-x^{\prime})\wedge K
for some constant
K>0
(i.e., the truncation of
x-x^{\prime}
to some bounded interval
[-K,K]
).

Each of the above examples of kernels
\psi
satisfies the required anti-monotonicity property (
3
). In fact, we may take any such anti-monotonic kernel to be also anti-symmetric. To see this, observe that we can always write

\psi(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\bigl\{\psi(x,x^{\prime})+\psi(x^{\prime},x)\bigr\}+\frac{1}{2}\bigl\{\psi(x,x^{\prime})-\psi(x^{\prime},x)\bigr\}=:\psi_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,x^{\prime})+\psi_{\mathrm{anti}}(x,x^{\prime}),

where by construction,
\psi_{\mathrm{sym}}
is a symmetric function while
\psi_{\mathrm{anti}}
is anti-symmetric. The function
\psi_{\mathrm{sym}}
does not contribute to the
p
-value—that is, the
p
-value would be unchanged if we replace
\psi
with
\psi_{\mathrm{anti}}
(for example, our test with the kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
coincides with the special case of linear test statistic from Section
2
). This establishes our claim, and henceforth we therefore assume without loss of generality that our anti-monotonic kernel
\psi
is also anti-symmetric.

3.2
Oracle strategies for choosing the weights and matching

We now build intuition for how to choose the weight vector
\mathbf{w}
and matching
M
effectively by sketching the asymptotics of our test, assuming some oracle knowledge (or estimates) of the data distribution; see Section
4
for a more formal analysis. Let us consider any statistic
T
of the form (
11
), where (as discussed above) without loss of generality we can take
\psi
to be an anti-symmetric function.
We now wish to choose the weights
\mathbf{w}=(w_{\ell})_{\ell\in[L]}
and matching
M=\{(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[L]}
to maximize the power of our test.
Given the data
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, the reference statistic
T_{\mathbf{s}}
can be expressed as
T_{\mathbf{s}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}s_{\ell}\cdot w_{\ell}\psi(x_{i_{\ell}},x_{j_{\ell}})
by construction of the swapping operation, and is therefore a sum of
L
independent random variables.
Under some regularity conditions on the weights
\mathbf{w}
and the function
\psi
, a central limit theorem (CLT) approximation gives, for large
L
, that

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\approx 1-\Phi(\hat{T})\qquad\textnormal{where}\qquad\hat{T}=\hat{T}(\mathbf{w},M):=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})^{2}}},

(12)

where
\Phi
is the standard Gaussian distribution function. The above approximation holds for fixed
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
and relies only on the CLT approximation for a weighted sum of
L
independent signs
s_{1},\dots,s_{L}\in\{\pm 1\}
. We should therefore aim to choose weights that
maximize
the approximate probability of rejection,
\mathbb{P}\bigl\{1-\Phi(\hat{T})\leq\alpha\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\bigr\}
, in order to achieve the best possible power. Under some conditions this conditional power can be further approximated as

\Phi\Biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}\mathrm{Var}\left({\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)}}\Biggr)

(13)

(see Theorem
B.1
in the Appendix for a closer look at this approximation).
The following lemma shows how to maximize this approximation over the weights, treating the matching
M
as fixed.

Lemma 4
.

Assume that
\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\psi_{\ell}(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\bigr)>0
for
\ell\in[L]
. Considered as a function of
\mathbf{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{L})\in[0,\infty)^{L}
, the function in (
13
) is maximized by the choice

w^{*}_{\ell}=\frac{\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right],0\right\}}{\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\psi_{\ell}(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\bigr)},

(14)

for
\ell\in[L]
.

Proof.

Writing
u_{\ell}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]
and
v_{\ell}=\bigl[\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\bigr)\bigr]^{1/2}
for convenience, for any
w\in[0,\infty)^{L}
we calculate

\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}u_{\ell}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}v_{\ell}^{2}}}\leq\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}\max\{u_{\ell},0\}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}v_{\ell}^{2}}}\\
=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(w_{\ell}v_{\ell})\cdot\frac{\max\{u_{\ell},0\}}{v_{\ell}}}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(w_{\ell}v_{\ell})^{2}}}\leq\biggl(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{\max\{u_{\ell},0\}^{2}}{v_{\ell}^{2}}\biggr)^{1/2},

with equality if and only if
w_{\ell}\propto\max\{u_{\ell},0\}/v_{\ell}^{2}
for
\ell\in[L]
.
∎

Using a plug-in estimate for the moments.

In Lemma
4
, the oracle weight vector
\mathbf{w}^{*}
depends on the conditional expected value and variance,
\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]
and
\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})\bigm|\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\bigr)
. For the remainder of this subsection, we will assume that we have access to estimates
\hat{E}_{ij}
of
\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]
and
\hat{V}_{ij}>0
of
\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\psi(X_{i},X_{j})\bigm|\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\bigr)
, for each
i,j\in[n]
, constructed from data that are independent of
\mathbf{X}
. For example, these might be obtained from a fitted model for the conditional distribution of
X
given
Y,Z
based on a separate dataset (e.g. via sample splitting). For the linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
, this would amount to estimating the first two moments of
X_{i}\mid Y_{i},Z_{i}
for
i\in[n]
, whereas if
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=\textnormal{sign}(x-x^{\prime})
, then we would need to estimate
\mathbb{P}\left\{{X_{i}>X_{j}}\ \middle|\ {Y_{i},Z_{i},Y_{j},Z_{j}}\right\}
for distinct indices
i,j
. With these estimates in place, we can seek to maximize the power of our test by choosing weights

\hat{w}_{\ell}=\frac{\max\{\hat{E}_{i_{\ell}j_{\ell}},0\}}{\hat{V}_{i_{\ell}j_{\ell}}}

for
\ell\in[L]
. We emphasise that even highly inaccurate estimates of the oracle weights still yield a valid test of
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, though accurate estimates of the conditional moments above enhance the power of our test. This is in contrast to methods of type (b) and (c) from the introduction, where the quality of estimates of (aspects of) the conditional distribution of
X
given
(Y,Z)
, or of
X
given
Z
, affects their Type I error control.

Choosing the matching.

If the oracle weights in (
14
) were known, then the estimator (
13
) of the test’s conditional power
would be maximized by solving a maximum-weight matching problem, namely that of finding

\displaystyle M^{*}\in\argmax_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})}\sum_{(i,j)\in M}\bigl(W_{ij}^{*}\bigr)^{2}\ \ \textnormal{where}\ \ W_{ij}^{*}=\frac{\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i},X_{j})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right],0\right\}}{\mathrm{Var}\left({\psi(X_{i},X_{j})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)}.

(15)

We refer to this version of our procedure as
oracle matching
.
In practice, when the moments of the distribution are estimated rather than known, we can instead use the plug-in estimates

\displaystyle\hat{M}\in\argmax_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})}\sum_{(i,j)\in M}\hat{W}_{ij}^{2}\ \ \textnormal{where}\ \hat{W}_{ij}=\frac{\max\{\hat{E}_{ij},0\}}{\hat{V}_{ij}}.

(16)

To run
PairSwap-ICI
, we then take weights
w_{\ell}=\hat{W}_{i_{\ell}j_{\ell}}
for each pair
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\in\hat{M}
.
This plug-in matching
\hat{M}
can be computed efficiently in polynomial time
(
Edmonds, 1965
;
Duan and Pettie, 2014
)
using an algorithm of
Gabow (1985)
.

3.3
Heuristic strategies for choosing the matching and weights

In Section
3.2
above, the test statistic
T(\mathbf{x})
was designed with the aim of maximizing the power of our test, and we argue in Theorem
9
in Section
4
that this strategy is particularly effective when we can construct good estimates of the first two conditional moments of our data. This may be possible if, for instance, we have been able to fit a model using a separate dataset; naturally, an accurate estimate of the true model can enable a very powerful test (since we have a good approximation of the alternative that we are testing against). The aim of this section, on the other hand, is to propose an alternative approach designed for settings when we may not be able to estimate reliably the required conditional moments, or because the computations are too costly (in particular, the matching strategy in (
16
)). We demonstrate a simple scheme for designing a weight vector
\mathbf{w}
, and two easy strategies for choosing a matching
M
, that do not require extensive prior knowledge or costly calculations. Of course, this will come at some cost in terms of the resulting power of the test, since we are no longer mimicking an oracle test—but, as we will see in both our theoretical guarantees and our empirical results below, these simple strategies can often attain high power nonetheless.

Throughout this section, we will restrict our attention to the univariate setting where
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
, and will also choose the kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
when defining our test statistic as in (
11
). We will work in the setting where we hypothesise that, under the alternative, there is a
positive
association between
X
and
Y
even after controlling for
Z
(of course, if our hypothesis is a negative association, we can follow an analogous strategy). The idea is simple: we will take pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
such that

•

Z_{i_{\ell}}\leq Z_{j_{\ell}}
(as required for validity), but
Z_{i_{\ell}}\approx Z_{j_{\ell}}
; and

•

Y_{i_{\ell}}>Y_{j_{\ell}}

Here, the choice
Z_{i_{\ell}}\approx Z_{j_{\ell}}
is desirable to ensure that the joint conditional distributions of
(X_{i_{\ell}},Y_{i_{\ell}})
given
Z_{i_{\ell}}
and
(X_{j_{\ell}},Y_{j_{\ell}})
given
Z_{j_{\ell}}
should be similar. In this case, it is anticipated that under
H_{0}^{\mathrm{ICI}}
, we should observe
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}})\approx 0
, and since
Y_{i_{\ell}}>Y_{j_{\ell}}
for each
\ell\in[L]
, if
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}})\gg 0
, then this provides evidence against
H_{0}^{\mathrm{ICI}}
. On the other hand, if we were to choose pairs with
Z_{i_{\ell}}\ll Z_{j_{\ell}}
, then these joint conditional distributions may be rather different, and the stochastic monotonicity assumption might mean that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}(X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}})\gg 0
occurs only rarely even when the conditional independence is violated. In other words, if we choose pairs with
Z_{i_{\ell}}\ll Z_{j_{\ell}}
, then under the stochastic monotonicity assumption, we retain Type I error control, but lose power relative to choosing pairs with
Z_{i_{\ell}}\approx Z_{j_{\ell}}
.

3.3.1
A simple weighting scheme

We begin by defining a mechanism for choosing the weights: we will take

w_{\ell}=\big(Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}}\big)_{+},

which is simply the difference in
Y
values, whenever this difference is positive for the pair
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
.

Why is this simple strategy a reasonable choice across broad settings? With this choice of weights, our test statistic
T(\mathbf{X})
is then given by

T(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{\ell:Y_{i_{\ell}}>Y_{j_{\ell}}}(Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}})\cdot(X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}}).

Since under the alternative, we expect
X_{i_{\ell}}>X_{j_{\ell}}
whenever
Y_{i_{\ell}}>Y_{j_{\ell}}
, this means that the expected value of
T(\mathbf{X})
is positive under the alternative (but, of course, is non-positive under the null).

As another motivation, consider the partially linear model where

\mathbb{E}\left[{X}\ \middle|\ {Y=y,Z=z}\right]=\beta^{*}y+\mu^{*}_{Z}(z),

and the conditional variance is constant,

\mathrm{Var}\left({X}\ \middle|\ {Y=y,Z=z}\right)=\sigma^{*}{}^{2}.

Then, following the oracle strategy of Section
3.2
, as in (
14
) we have
oracle weights

\displaystyle w^{*}_{\ell}=\frac{\max\{\mathbb{E}\left[{X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right],0\}}{\mathrm{Var}\left({X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\max\bigl\{\beta^{*}(Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}})+\bigl(\mu^{*}_{Z}(Z_{i_{\ell}})-\mu^{*}_{Z}(Z_{j_{\ell}})\bigr),0\bigr\}}{2\sigma^{*}{}^{2}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\beta^{*}}{2\sigma^{*}{}^{2}}\max\bigl\{(Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}}),0\bigr\},

where the last step holds since we have assumed
Z_{i_{\ell}}\approx Z_{j_{\ell}}
in our choice of the pair, and so we can expect
\mu^{*}_{Z}(Z_{i_{\ell}})\approx\mu^{*}_{Z}(Z_{j_{\ell}})
, at least when
\mu_{Z}^{*}
is smooth. But crucially, our test is invariant to rescaling the weights—that is, choosing weights
w_{\ell}=\max\bigl\{Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}},0\bigr\}
is equivalent to choosing weights
\frac{\beta^{*}}{2\sigma^{*}{}^{2}}\max\bigl\{(Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}}),0\bigr\}
, and thus is nearly equivalent to the oracle weights.

3.3.2
Two simple matching schemes

Next, we propose two matching strategies that, again, do not require any knowledge or estimate of the model.

Neighbor matching.

Our first simple matching strategy is to choose pairs that are nearest neighbors in the list of sorted
Z
values, as formalized in Algorithm
1
. This strategy ensures that, for all pairs
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})=\bigl(\pi(2m-1),\pi(2m)\bigr)
that are included in the matching, we have
Z_{i_{\ell}}\leq Z_{j_{\ell}}
and
Y_{i_{\ell}}>Y_{j_{\ell}}
by construction, and moreover, it likely holds that
Z_{i_{\ell}}\approx Z_{j_{\ell}}
(since we have chosen two consecutive
Z
values in the sorted list).

Algorithm 1

Neighbor matching

Preliminaries:
sort
Z
values, i.e., find a permutation
\pi
of
[n]
such that

Z_{\pi(1)}\leq Z_{\pi(2)}\leq\dots\leq Z_{\pi(n-1)}\leq Z_{\pi(n)}.

for

m=1,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor

do

If
Y_{\pi(2m-1)}>Y_{\pi(2m)}
, then add the new pair
\bigl(\pi(2m-1),\pi(2m)\bigr)
to the matching.

end

for

However, an obvious limitation of this naïve matching strategy is that many consecutive pairs
\bigl(\pi(2m-1),\pi(2m)\bigr)
in the sorted list can fail to have
Y_{\pi(2m-1)}>Y_{\pi(2m)}
just by chance. In particular, if the
Z
values in this pair are approximately equal (as we might expect), then the values
Y_{\pi(2m-1)},Y_{\pi(2m)}
are expected to be approximately independent and identically distributed—which means that the event
Y_{\pi(2m-1)}>Y_{\pi(2m)}
will fail approximately half the time. In other words, we are discarding approximately half of the data: we will expect to have
L\approx n/4
pairs in this matching (meaning that
2L\approx n/2
data points have been assigned to a matched pair).

Cross-bin matching.

Our next strategy is cross-bin matching, which aims to avoid the inefficiency of neighbor matching by using (nearly) all of the data.
To implement this strategy, we will partition the list of sorted
Z
values into
K
bins, and will allow a pair of data points to be matched as long as the
Z
values are in adjacent bins (rather than requiring consecutive
Z
values, as for neighbor matching).

Algorithm 2

Cross-bin matching

Preliminaries:
sort
Z
values, i.e., find a permutation
\pi
of
[n]
such that

Z_{\pi(1)}\leq Z_{\pi(2)}\leq\dots\leq Z_{\pi(n-1)}\leq Z_{\pi(n)},

and define
K
bins of indices,

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\pi(1),\dots,\pi(m)\},

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\pi(m+1),\dots,\pi(2m)\},

\displaystyle A_{K}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\pi\bigl((K-1)m+1\bigr),\dots,\pi(Km)\bigr\},

where
m=\lfloor n/K\rfloor
.

for

k=1,\dots,K

do

Define
r_{k,1},\dots,r_{k,m}
as a permutation of
A_{k}
such that

Y_{r_{k,1}}\leq\dots\leq Y_{r_{k,m}}
.

end

for

for

k=1,\dots,K-1

do

for

s=1,\dots,\lfloor m/2\rfloor

do

If
Y_{r_{k,m+1-s}}>Y_{r_{k+1,s}}
, then add the new pair
(r_{k,m+1-s},r_{k+1,s})
to the matching.

end

for

end

for

To explain this procedure in words:

•

First we group the
Z
values into
K
bins, with each bin
A_{k}
containing
m=\lfloor n/K\rfloor
consecutive
Z
values.

•

We then attempt to match the largest values of
Y
in bin
k
with the smallest values of
Y
in bin
k+1
—that is, in the inner “for loop” in Algorithm
2
, at step
s=1
we are attempting to match the largest
Y
value in bin
k
(i.e.,
Y_{r_{k,m}}
) with the smallest
Y
value in bin
k+1
(i.e.,
Y_{r_{k+1,1}}
), and then at step
s=2
we proceed to matching the second-largest and second-smallest, and so on.

This scheme is illustrated in Figure
1
, and is stated formally in Algorithm
2
.

Figure 1:
Demonstration of the cross-bin matching scheme described in
Algorithm

2
. The dashed lines indicate the median within each bin.

Why do we expect that this strategy will be more powerful than the simpler neighbor matching strategy? At a high level, while neighbor matching is expected to discard around half of the data, the cross-bin matching strategy can potentially assign nearly all data points to a matched pair. However, there is a potential tradeoff: while the pairs produced by both strategies will likely satisfy
Z_{i_{\ell}}\approx Z_{j_{\ell}}
, this approximation will be closer to equality for neighbor matching (where pairs consist of consecutive
Z
values) than cross-bin matching (where pairs consist of
Z
values in neighboring bins, i.e., they may be up to
2m
positions apart in the sorted list). If
m
is not too large (i.e., the number of bins
K
is not too small), though, then we may hope that this difference is negligible.
We will examine both methods theoretically in the following section to study these tradeoffs in more detail.

4
Power analysis

In this section, we study the power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test under the following general model. The data
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})_{i\in[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{X,Y,Z}
are drawn according to

\mathbf{X}=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})+\sigma_{n}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},

(17)

with
(Y_{i},Z_{i})_{i\in[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Y,Z}
drawn independently from
(\zeta_{i})_{i\in[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{\zeta}
. We assume that
\mu_{n}:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
(applied componentwise) is a measurable function and
\sigma_{n}>0
, and also that
P_{\zeta}
has mean
0
, variance
1
, and finite fourth moment. Throughout this section,
we assume that the statistic
T
admits the form in (
11
) with
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
.

4.1
Asymptotic power guarantee for the general
PairSwap-ICI
test

We start by studying the power of
PairSwap-ICI
test in the setting where
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
are general measurable spaces. We use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote the Euclidean norm, and write
\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}
for the total variation (TV) distance, so that for probability measures
P
and
Q
defined on a common measurable space
(W,\mathcal{W})
,
\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(P,Q):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{W}}|P(A)-Q(A)|
. Given any vector
\bf{v}
, we write
\bf{v}^{+}
to denote the vector with
i
th component
v_{i}^{+}=\max\{v_{i},0\}
. We write
\bf{a}\circ\bf{b}
for the Hadamard product of vectors
\bf{a}
,
\bf{b}
of the same dimension, with
i
th component
a_{i}\cdot b_{i}
.
Given a matching
M=\bigl((i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\bigr)_{\ell\in[L]}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
and a vector
\mathbf{v}=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the differencing operator
\Delta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{L}
by

\Delta\mathbf{v}\equiv(\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{v})_{\ell\in[L]}:=(v_{i_{\ell}}-v_{j_{\ell}})_{\ell\in[L]}.

In particular, it will be convenient to define the
mean difference vector

\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\equiv\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)=\bigl(\mu_{n}(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}})-\mu_{n}(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}})\bigr)_{\ell\in[L]}\in\mathbb{R}^{L}.

We will make the following assumptions on the weight vector
\mathbf{w}\equiv\mathbf{w}_{n}\in[0,\infty)^{L}
and mean difference vector:

Assumption A1
.

We have
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}>0

and
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}={\rm o}_{P}(\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2})
.

Assumption A2
.

We have
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}={\rm o}_{P}\left(\big|\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\big|\vee\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\right)
and
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}=\mathrm{o}_{P}\left(\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\vee\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\right)
.

To explain the origin of these assumptions, observe that since we are under the model class (
17
), and we use the shared linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
, the
PairSwap-ICI
test revolves around the statistic

\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})+\sigma_{n}\bigl(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\bigr);

see (
31
). Here, the first term can be interpreted as the signal of the test, while the second term represents the additional noise. Under this interpretation, Assumption
A1
ensures that the noise term is well behaved by requiring that the weight vector
\mathbf{w}
is not too spiky, while Assumption
A2
requires the signal term not to be too spiky.

The following theorem gives our main general result on the asymptotic conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose that the triple
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
satisfies (
17
). Suppose further that the weight vector
\mathbf{w}\in[0,\infty)^{L}
and
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
(which may both depend on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
but not
\mathbf{X}
) satisfy Assumptions
A1
and
A2
. Then for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have that

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\biggr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Theorem
5
follows from Theorem
B.1
in Appendix
B.1
, which provides finite-sample upper and lower bounds on the conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test for any feasible matching and weight vector. One key insight of Theorem
5
is that it completely characterizes the asymptotic power of our test through the
PairSwap-ICI
signal-to-noise ratio

S(\mathbf{w},M):=\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}.

(18)

It is interesting to compare the conclusion of Theorem
5
with the informal approximation to the conditional power given by (
13
). Referring to the terms in that expression, in the model (
17
), we have

\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}\mathrm{Var}\left({\psi(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)}}=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[{X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]}{\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}\mathrm{Var}\left({X_{i_{\ell}}-X_{j_{\ell}}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)}}=S(\mathbf{w},M).

Thus, we can regard Theorem
5
as formalizing the heuristic arguments in Section
3.2
. By analogy with Lemma
4
, we see that the oracle weight vector maximizes the correlation between
\mathbf{w}
and
\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, and is achieved by setting

\mathbf{w}^{*}\propto\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}).

(19)

4.2
Hardness of testing the null
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}

We now turn to the question of the fundamental hardness of testing
H_{0}^{\mathrm{ICI}}
in the model (
17
).

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
denote the closed convex cone of nondecreasing functions on
\mathcal{Z}
, i.e. the set of
f:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(z_{1})\leq f(z_{2})
whenever
z_{1}\preceq z_{2}
. Define

\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}=\frac{\inf_{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}}\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}^{n}}}\left[{\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}\right]}{\sigma_{n}},\quad\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}=\frac{\inf_{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}}\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}},

(20)

which we call the oracle and empirical
isotonic signal-to-noise ratio
respectively (here
g(\mathbf{Z})
is again applied componentwise). These quantities capture the extent to which the true model for
X\mid Y,Z
contains signal that cannot be explained by the isotonic null,
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. In particular, under
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, we have
\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}=\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}=0
(since we can simply take
g
to be the true regression function, which does not depend on
Y
), while under the alternative these quantities might be large. We naturally expect
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\approx\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}
when
n
is large.

We can relate the
PairSwap-ICI
signal-to-noise ratio (
18
) with oracle weight vector
\mathbf{w}^{*}
from (
19
) to the empirical isotonic signal-to-noise ratio via

S(\mathbf{w}^{*},M)=\frac{\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}}\leq\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n};

(21)

see Theorem
B.3
for a proof.

The goal of this subsection is to show that the population counterpart of
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}
, i.e. the oracle isotonic signal-to-noise ratio
\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}
, provides a fundamental limit to the power of
any
test of
H_{0}^{\mathrm{ICI}}
. To this end, we begin with a standard total variation calculation.

Proposition 6
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Fix any test
3
3

3

Traditionally, we think of a hypothesis test
\phi
as a map from data to a decision, i.e.,
\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\in\{0,1\}
. Why, then, do we define tests
\phi
as mapping to the space
[0,1]
? This is because, in some settings, we may want to consider randomized tests—for instance, in the notation above where
\phi
maps to
[0,1]
, an outcome
\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=0.75
represents that, given the data, our randomized test rejects the null with probability 0.75. Of course, a nonrandomized test is simply a special case, obtained by restricting the output of
\phi
to lie in
\{0,1\}
.

\phi:(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})^{n}\to[0,1]
that controls the Type I error at level
\alpha
, i.e.,

\sup_{P\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}}\mathbb{E}_{{P}}\left[{\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right]\leq\alpha.

Then for any distribution
P_{X,Y,Z}
,

\mathbb{E}_{{P_{X,Y,Z}}}\left[{\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right]\leq\alpha+\inf_{Q_{X,Y,Z}\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}}\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}\bigl(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z}\bigr).

This last total variation term is the distance of
P_{X,Y,Z}
from the null class
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, meaning the closer
P
is to null models, the harder it will be to have non-trivial power against
P
. While in general it is hard to derive exact expressions for the total variation term, under some additional model assumptions on
P
, we can derive interpretable upper bounds for it. Specifically, consider the model (
17
) where the noise distribution
P_{\zeta}
has a density function
f_{\zeta}
satisfying

\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\sqrt{f_{\zeta}(x)}-\sqrt{f_{\zeta}(x+t)}\right)^{2}\;\mathsf{d}x\right\}^{1/2}\leq L_{\zeta}|t|\textnormal{ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$.}

(22)

Note that the left-hand side is equal to

{\rm H}(P_{\zeta},t+P_{\zeta}),

where
{\rm H}
denotes the Hellinger distance between distributions, and
t+P_{\zeta}
denotes the distribution of
t+\zeta
when
\zeta\sim P_{\zeta}
(i.e., a translation of the original distribution
P_{\zeta}
). In other words, we are assuming that the map
t\mapsto t+P_{\zeta}
is
L_{\zeta}
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hellinger distance. For example, if
P_{\zeta}=\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is the standard Gaussian, then
{\rm H}^{2}(P_{\zeta},t+P_{\zeta})=2\bigl(1-e^{-t^{2}/8}\bigr)\leq t^{2}/4

(
Pardo, 2018
)
, and so the assumption (
22
) holds with
L_{\zeta}=1/2
.

We now state a result establishing a bound on power for any test, as a function of the isotonic SNR.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
satisfy (
17
) for some noise distribution
P_{\zeta}
satisfying the condition (
22
). Then

\inf_{Q_{X,Y,Z}\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}}\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}\left(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z}\right)\leq L_{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{ISNR}_{n},

and consequently, for any test
\phi

that controls Type I error at level
\alpha
,

\mathbb{E}_{{P_{X,Y,Z}}}\left[{\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right]\leq\alpha+L_{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}.

To summarize, we have shown that under a mild assumption on the noise distribution
P_{\zeta}
, the oracle isotonic SNR gives a universal upper bound on the power of
any
test of the isotonic null,
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
—no valid test can achieve non-trivial power when
\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}
is negligible.

4.3
Asymptotic power of oracle and plug-in procedures

The theory of Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, and in particular the general inequality (
21
), raise the natural question of the extent to which the
PairSwap-ICI
test, with appropriate choices of weight vector and matching, is able to approximate the upper bound on the power of any valid test, as articulated by Theorem
7
. Ignoring the difference between the empirical and oracle isotonic SNRs, this amounts to asking about the tightness of the inequality (
21
). We address this by specializing Theorem
5
initially to the setting of oracle weights and matching, as well as
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
. Since these oracle weights and matching depend on properties of the underlying data generating distribution that would typically be unknown to the practitioner, we then establish that the same asymptotic power properties hold for the plug-in version of the procedure outlined in Section
3.2
.

4.3.1
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}
drives the conditional power of oracle matching

Theorem
8
below provides asymptotic upper and lower bounds on the conditional power
\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}
for oracle weights and matching.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose that
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}>0
and that
\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\sigma_{n}\bigr)\vee\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\sigma_{n}\bigr)^{4}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n})
. Then, under the model (
17
), the conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure with oracle weights and matching satisfies for
\alpha\in(0,1)
that

\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}}{\sqrt{2}}\biggr)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\Phi\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

(23)

The condition
\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\sigma_{n}\bigr)\vee\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\sigma_{n}\bigr)^{4}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n})
is a mild assumption on the quality of the matching. For example, if
\mu_{n}
is bounded and
\sigma_{n}=\sigma
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\sigma_{n}\bigr)\vee\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\sigma_{n}\bigr)^{4}=\mathrm{O}(1)
, so for this assumption to hold, it is sufficient to have
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\overset{P}{\to}\infty
; since this latter quantity represents the Euclidean distance between the
n
-dimensional vector
\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
and the convex cone
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
, this amounts to a weak requirement.

From Theorem
8
, we see that the empirical isotonic SNR drives both the upper and lower bound on the conditional power. Furthermore, the upper and lower bounds on conditional power match, up to the factor of
1/\sqrt{2}
appearing in the expression for the lower bound but not the upper bound. In Theorem
12
in Section
4.4
below, we will see that in a partially linear model setting and under the hypothesis of the symmetry of the distribution
P_{Y|Z}
, it is possible to close this gap.

4.3.2
Power guarantees for plug-in matching with an estimate of
\mu_{n}

Suppose now that no oracle information is available and that we run the
PairSwap-ICI
test using the plug-in matching from Section
4.3.2
. By (
19
), under the model (
17
) with the linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
, and since the
p
-value does not depend on the scale of the weights, we may assume without loss of generality that the optimal weight vector is

\mathbf{w}^{*}=\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}).

In particular, the oracle weights (and hence matching) depend only on
\mu_{n}
and not on
\sigma_{n}
. Thus, we may construct an estimate
\hat{\mu}_{n}
of
\mu_{n}
on a random split of the data, and then apply
PairSwap-ICI
on the remaining observations with the plug-in matching
\hat{M}
from (
16
). For simplicity of presentation, however, in Theorem
9
below, we assume that
\hat{\mu}_{n}
is constructed based on an independent dataset.

Theorem 9
.

Consider the setting and assumptions of Theorem
8
, except that we use plug-in matching
\hat{M}
with an estimate
\hat{\mu}_{n}
constructed based on independent data. Suppose further that

\frac{\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{4}}{\sigma_{n}^{4}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}),

with the probability statements taken over the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
and the data used to construct
\hat{\mu}_{n}
. Then the conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
satisfies (
23
) for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Thus, under suitable consistency assumptions on
\hat{\mu}_{n}
, we can recover the power guarantees in Theorem
8
. Since the requirements on
\hat{\mu}_{n}
in Theorem
9
are quite weak, it may be advantageous to employ a data split of unequal sizes when using plug-in matching—in particular, we might choose to use only a small fraction of the available data points to estimate
\mu_{n}
and compute the weights and matching, and save the rest for the
PairSwap-ICI
test once these choices are fixed.

4.4
Near-optimal power guarantees without knowledge of
\mu_{n}

hile the results of Section
4.3
establish the optimality properties of the oracle and plug-in versions of the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure, we remark that when
n
is large, it may be time-consuming to compute the optimal matching in (
15
). The aim of this subsection, then is to show that the very simple matching schemes of Section
3.3
enjoy similar optimality properties, at least under some further assumptions on the model (
17
). Specifically, assume here that
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
and consider the class of partially linear Gaussian models given by

(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\penalty\ \textnormal{satisfy}\penalty\ \eqref{model:general alternative}\ \textnormal{with}\penalty\ \mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})+\beta_{n}\,\mathbf{Y}

(24)

for some
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
(applied componentwise) and some
\beta_{n}\in(0,\infty)
; we assume also that
Y
is a mean-zero variable taking values in
[-1,1]
, with
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}\left({Y}\ \middle|\ {Z}\right)}\right]>0
(i.e.,
Y
is not equal to a deterministic function of
Z
).
Note that
\mu_{0}
is a fixed function, so the dependence on
n
of the distribution of
(X,Y,Z)
is solely through
\beta_{n}
and
\sigma_{n}
. The following lemma relates
\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}
to a more interpretable quantity, namely the expected conditional variance of
Y
given
Z
.

Lemma 10
.

Under the model class (
24
), provided that
\mathbb{E}|\mu_{0}(Z)|<\infty
,

\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}\left({Y}\ \middle|\ {Z}\right)}\right]\bigr)^{1/2}\,\bigl(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)\leq\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\bigl(\mathrm{Var}(Y)\bigr)^{1/2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}.

We can draw several conclusions from this result.
By Lemma
10
and Theorem
7
, when
P_{\zeta}=\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, no valid test
can achieve asymptotically non-trivial power against an alternative in the model class (
24
) when
\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}=\mathrm{o}(1)
. On the other hand, if
\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\to\infty
, then
\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}\to\infty
, and the
PairSwap-ICI
test should have power converging to
1
.

Under mild conditions on the model, we expect that
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}=\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}\bigl(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)
. With this approximation, another consequence of Lemma
10
and (
23
) is that the conditional power of oracle matching also satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq\Phi\Biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}(Y)\bigr]\bigr\}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}}\Biggr)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

(25)

We can now compare the asymptotic conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test with neighbor matching and cross-bin matching against this lower bound.

Theorem 11
.

Under the model class (
24
), fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
and assume that
\sigma_{n}\gg\sqrt{\log n/n}
. Assume also that
\mu_{0}(Z)
is a sub-Gaussian random variable. Then the conditional power of neighbor matching (
Algorithm

1
), implemented with kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
and weights
w_{\ell}=\max\bigl\{Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}},0\bigr\}
, satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}(Y)\bigr]\bigr\}^{1/2}}{2\sigma_{n}}\biggr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Notably, the conditional power of neighbor matching matches the lower bound from (
25
) up to a factor of
1/\sqrt{2}
in the signal-to-noise ratio term. This factor arises due to the inherent inefficiency of neighbor matching: as discussed in Section
3.3
, neighbor matching discards roughly half the data.

We now turn to cross-bin matching. Since we would like paired
Z
values
Z_{i_{\ell}}\leq Z_{j_{\ell}}
to be approximately equal (in order to avoid an overly conservative test), we further allow the number of bins
K
to depend on sample size
n
, and write this as
K_{n}
from now on. Consequently, the power of cross-bin matching can meet the lower bound in (
25
).

Theorem 12
.

Under the same setting and assumptions as Theorem
11
, the conditional power of cross-bin matching (Algorithm
2
) with
K_{n}
bins (where
\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sqrt{n\log n}\ll K_{n}\ll n
), implemented with kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
and weights
w_{\ell}=\max\bigl\{Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}},0\bigr\}
, satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}(Y)\bigr]\bigr\}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}}\biggr)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

(26)

Further, if the conditional distribution

P_{Y\mid Z}
is symmetric about its mean almost surely, then the conditional power of cross-bin matching satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}(Y)\bigr]\bigr\}^{1/2}}{\sigma_{n}}\biggr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

In particular, the first part of the result shows that for cross-bin matching, the conditional power asymptotically matches the lower bound in (
25
), without an additional factor of
1/\sqrt{2}
as for neighbor matching under the same assumptions. Moreover, with an additional assumption of symmetry of the conditional distribution
P_{Y\mid Z}
, we can also remove another factor of
1/\sqrt{2}
. Finally, in Theorem
B.2
in Appendix
B.5
, we characterize the asymptotic conditional power of cross-bin matching (in contrast to the lower bound in Theorem
12
) even without the symmetry assumption.

5
Extension to multivariate response spaces

So far we have worked under Assumption
1
and in the setting where the response space
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
. In this section, we extend the
PairSwap-ICI
framework to multivariate responses, allowing
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
with
d_{X}\geq 1
. This extension broadens the applicability of our method, enabling it to handle settings in which the response variable is vector-valued, or other settings where there is no natural response variable and
X
is vector-valued.

We begin by generalizing the stochastic monotonicity assumption to the case
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
. For
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
, we write
x\leq y
to denote the coordinate-wise ordering
x_{j}\leq y_{j}
for all
j\in[d_{X}]
, and we say that a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is nondecreasing if
f(x)\leq f(y)
whenever
x\leq y
.

Assumption 2

(Monotonicity of the conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}
)
.

Let
\preceq
be a partial order on
\mathcal{Z}
. We assume that
X
is stochastically nondecreasing in
Z
, meaning that for all nondecreasing functions
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\textnormal{if $z\preceq z^{\prime}$ then }\mathbb{E}_{{P}}\left[{f(X)}\ \middle|\ {Z=z}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{{P}}\left[{f(X)}\ \middle|\ {Z=z^{\prime}}\right].

In particular, Assumption
2
specializes to Assumption
1
when
d_{X}=1
.
4
4

4

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the function
y\mapsto{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{y\geq x}\right\}
is nondecreasing, so Assumption
2
imposes a monotonicity condition on the upper orthant probabilities of
X\mid Z
. When
d_{X}=1
, this reduces to monotonicity of the conditional survival function, as in Assumption
1
.

As before, to circumvent the hardness of conditional independence testing, we consider the restricted null hypothesis

\displaystyle H_{0,d_{X}}^{\textnormal{ICI}}:X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z,\textnormal{ and $P_{X\mid Z}$ satisfies Assumption\penalty\ \ref{asm:st_gen},}

(27)

so that
H_{0,1}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
coincides with
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
.

The only modification of the
PairSwap-ICI
methodology required to accommodate multivariate
X
concerns the definition of anti-monotonic functions. Specifically, a function
\psi:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is now said to satisfy anti-monotonicity if

\displaystyle\psi(x+\Delta,x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime})-\psi(x^{\prime}-\Delta^{\prime},x+\Delta)\geq\psi(x,x^{\prime})-\psi(x^{\prime},x)

(28)

for all
x,x^{\prime}
and all
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
, where the inequality is interpreted elementwise. Otherwise, the testing procedure proceeds exactly as in Section
2
: given a matching
M=\bigl((i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{L},j_{L})\bigr)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
and anti-monotonic functions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{L}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we define the test statistic
T
as in (
4
), construct the
p
-value
p
as in (
5
), and reject
H_{0,d_{X}}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
at level
\alpha\in(0,1)
whenever
p\leq\alpha
.

Theorem
13
below confirms that the multivariate response
PairSwap-ICI
procedure retains finite-sample Type I error control.

Theorem 13
.

Under
H_{0,d_{X}}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, the conditional Type I error of the
PairSwap-ICI
test satisfies
\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\alpha
for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
.

The proof of Theorem
13
is analogous to that of Theorem
2
. For completeness, we give the proof in Appendix
C
. We emphasise again that
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
here may be arbitrary measurable spaces, provided that
\mathcal{Z}
is equipped with a partial order.

Following the methodological development of Section
3
, we consider anti-monotonic functions of the form
\psi_{\ell}=w_{\ell}\cdot\psi
with
w_{\ell}\geq 0
for all
\ell\in[L]
and an anti-monotonic kernel
\psi
. The oracle and plug-in matching schemes introduced in (
15
) and (
16
) can be used directly in this setting. The main practical challenge therefore lies in designing a kernel
\psi
that effectively captures the information contained in
\mathbf{X}
.

A natural approach is to project
X
along directions in the positive orthant. Specifically, writing
S^{+}_{d_{X}}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}:u_{j}\geq 0\penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ j\in[d_{X}],\,\|u\|_{2}=1\}
, we may take any
u\in S^{+}_{d_{X}}
and then consider the projection
\Pi_{d_{X}}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by
\Pi_{d_{X}}(x):=u^{\top}x
. In particular, the direction
u
can also be chosen based on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
to gain higher power. Any such projection reduces the multivariate response to a scalar quantity while preserving the required monotonicity structure. Consequently, we can re-use the anti-monotonic
\psi
functions introduced in Section
3
;
for the remainder of this section, we focus on the linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=\Pi_{d_{X}}(x)-\Pi_{d_{X}}(x^{\prime})
.

To study the asymptotic conditional power of the multivariate response
PairSwap-ICI
procedure, we assume that the data
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})_{i\in[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{X,Y,Z}
are drawn according to the model (
17
), where
\mu_{n}:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
(applied componentwise) is a measurable function and
P_{\zeta}
is a distribution on
\mathcal{X}
having mean
0
, ones on the diagonal of its covariance matrix and
\int_{\mathcal{X}}\|x\|_{2}^{4}\,dP_{\zeta}<\infty
. Here
\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{X}}
, and given the projection direction
u\in S_{d_{X}}^{+}
, we have

\Pi_{d_{X}}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{X}u=\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})u+\sigma_{n}\,{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\prime}

for some
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\prime}
with independent components having mean
0
, variance
1
and finite fourth moment. Therefore, conditional on
u
, an application of Theorem
5
immediately yields an analogous asymptotic characterization of the conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test:

Corollary 14
.

In the setting of Theorem
5
, suppose the weights, the matching and the projection direction
u
satisfy Assumptions
A1
and
A2
after replacing
\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
with
\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})u
. Then for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\bigl(\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})u\bigr)}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\biggr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Similar to (
19
), for a given matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
and a projection direction
u\in S^{+}_{d_{X}}
, the oracle weights are

\mathbf{w}^{\star}\propto\Delta\bigl(\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})u\bigr)
. With that choice, the
PairSwap-ICI
signal-to-noise ratio satisfies

S(\mathbf{w}^{*},M;u)=\bigl\|\Delta\bigl(\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})u\bigr)\bigr\|_{2},

and the optimal choice of
u
is

u^{*}\in\argmax_{u\in S_{d_{X}}^{+}}S(\mathbf{w}^{*},M;u).

In practice, the oracle weights may again be estimated using a plug-in approach, so that a numerical approximation to
u^{*}
(using either a deterministic or random grid) may be found.

6
Simulations

In this section, we evaluate the performance of our method on simulated data, and compare the matching strategies from Section
3.3
.
