Code for reproducing all experiments can be found at
https://github.com/jake-soloff/PairSwap-ICI-Experiments
.
We will examine two versions of the
PairSwap-ICI
test:

•

Neighbor matching (Algorithm
1
), with the linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
and weights
w_{\ell}=\max\{Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}},0\}
as discussed in Section
3.3
;

•

Cross-bin matching (Algorithm
2
) with
K_{n}=\left\lfloor n^{2/3}\right\rfloor
bins, again with the linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
and weights
w_{\ell}=\max\{Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}},0\}
. This particular choice of
K_{n}
is motivated by Theorem
12
, which suggests choosing the number of bins
K_{n}
to satisfy
\sqrt{n\log n}\ll K_{n}\ll n
, in order to achieve competitive power with neighbor matching.

6.1
Extent of conservativeness under
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}

Theorem

2
establishes valid, finite-sample Type I error control for our method. The purpose of this section is to evaluate the extent of the conservativeness of the Type I error under various null distributions. Since our inference relies on the fact that matched pairs
(X_{i_{\ell}},X_{j_{\ell}})
are stochastically ordered under the null, intuitively this should depend on the strength of the monotonicity in the conditional distribution.

To see how the dependence between
X
and
Z
affects the rejection probability, we sample
X
from an additive noise model

X\mid Y,Z\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(\mu(\gamma Z),1\bigr),

where
Y,Z
are independent standard normal random variables. As long as
\mu
is nondecreasing and
\gamma\geq 0
, this joint distribution belongs to the null
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. The scalar
\gamma
controls the strength of the monotonicity of
X\mid Z
. In particular, as
\gamma\searrow 0
we expect the Type I error
\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\right\}
to approach
\alpha
.

In our simulations, we consider two functions
\mu
, the identity
\mu(z)=z
and the standard Gaussian CDF
\mu(z)=\Phi(z)
.
Figure

2
shows the Type I error as a function of
\gamma
for two levels of
\alpha
. We observe similar results for each
\alpha
, where the test typically becomes more conservative as
\gamma
increases, as expected. Under the null, our test is more conservative for cross-bin matching than for neighbor matching, since the
Z
values are further apart in cross-bin matching. We remark that while other methods with asymptotic Type I error guarantees may exhibit substantial violations of Type I error control in finite samples, our procedure
PairSwap-ICI
test offers finite-sample validity.

Figure 2:
Simulation results illustrating Type I error control under the null
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
for two forms of the conditional mean
\mathbb{E}\left[{X}\ \middle|\ {Z}\right]
. Each subplot shows the rejection probability of the
PairSwap-ICI
test on a dataset of size
1000
, averaged over
10^{4}
simulation trials, as a function of the strength of stochastic monotonicity
\gamma
.

Figure 3:
Simulation results demonstrating the power of the
PairSwap-ICI
for two alternatives at level
\alpha=0.1
. Each subplot shows the rejection probability, averaged over
10^{4}
simulation trials, as a function of the sample size
n
.
Columns correspond to different relationships between
Y
and
Z
.
In each setting,
X
follows a Gaussian linear model with mean
\beta_{n}Y+\gamma Z
, where
\beta_{n}=n^{-1/3}
and
\gamma=0.5
(above) or
\gamma=2
(below).

6.2
Power under alternatives

In Section
4.4
we showed theoretically that our heuristic methods—neighbor matching and cross-bin matching—achieve high power (in fact, power that tends to 1) in the partial linear model (
24
) with
\sigma_{n}=1
for all
n
, provided the signal
\beta_{n}
exceeds the detection threshold
n^{-1/2}
. In this section, we now examine this setting empirically. We sample data from the Gaussian linear model

X\mid Y,Z\sim\mathcal{N}\bigl(\beta_{n}Y+\gamma Z,1\bigr),

with
\beta_{n}=n^{-1/3}
. The pair
(Y,Z)
is drawn from a bivariate Gaussian

\mathcal{N}_{2}\biggl(\boldsymbol{0},\begin{bmatrix}1&\rho_{YZ}\\
\rho_{YZ}&1\end{bmatrix}\biggr).

Figure

3
shows the (unconditional) power as a function of the sample size
n
for various choices of
\gamma
and
\rho_{YZ}
. In Setting 1, we set
\gamma=0.5
, and cross-bin matching uniformly dominates neighbor matching because it allows us to make many more matches of similar quality. On the other hand, in Setting 2 we set
\gamma=2
, so the strong dependence of
X
on
Z
means the quality of a match
(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})
degrades much more quickly as the gap
Z_{j_{\ell}}-Z_{i_{\ell}}
increases—that is, for cross-bin matching, where
Z_{i_{\ell}}
and
Z_{j_{\ell}}
may be farther apart than for neighbor matching, this gap may lead to conservativeness that results in a loss of power. However, with a sufficiently large sample size, cross-bin matching performs at least as well as the neighbor matching. This is because the bin width decreases as
n
increases, so the quality of the cross-bin matches rivals that of the neighbor matches (with many more matches). The dependence
\rho_{YZ}
between
Y
and
Z
does not have a major impact on the power of these two methods.

One take-away message from these simulations is that the stronger the stochastic monotonicity is believed to be, the more bins one should use in cross-bin matching, to avoid excessive conservativeness.
On the other hand, if the extent of stochastic monotonicity is believed to be weak, then one should use fewer bins in cross-bin matching, to allow for a greater number of matches as well as larger weights for the matched pairs, and thereby increase power.

6.3
Empirical performance in multivariate settings

We now assess the empirical performance of the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure when the variables
X,Y,Z
may all be multivariate, and we seek to test
H_{0,d_{X}}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. Fix
d_{X}=d_{Y}=5
and
d_{Z}\in\{2,3\}
and generate

Y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d_{Y}}),\qquad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma),

where
\Sigma
is an equicorrelation covariance matrix with
\Sigma_{jj}=1
and
\Sigma_{jk}=0.8
for
j\neq k
. Independently, we generate
\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d_{Y}})
.

We consider three data generating mechanisms for the response
X
, designed to capture different forms of conditional dependence between
X
and
Y
given
Z
. In each setting we study two regimes: a null case with
\beta=0
, corresponding to conditional independence, and an alternative case with
\beta=0.5
, where conditional dependence is present.

(i)

Setting 1 (Linear dependence):

X=\beta Y+0.1\sum_{j=1}^{d_{Z}}Z_{j}\,\mathbf{1}_{d_{Y}}+\zeta
.

(ii)

Setting 2 (Quadratic dependence):

X=\beta Y^{2}+0.1\sum_{j=1}^{d_{Z}}Z_{j}\,\mathbf{1}_{d_{Y}}+\zeta
.
where
Y^{2}
denotes the elementwise square of
Y
.

(iii)

Setting 3 (Interaction dependence):

X=(0.1+\beta Y)\sum_{j=1}^{d_{Z}}Z_{j}+\zeta
.

For each dataset, matched pairs are constructed using a simple random pairing strategy: observations are paired at random, and only those pairs satisfying the coordinate-wise order constraint on the
\mathcal{Z}
space are retained. These retained pairs form the matched sample used by the
PairSwap-ICI
test.
This matching scheme inevitably leads to a loss in effective sample size, since many
Z
pairs are not comparable under the coordinate-wise order. Nevertheless, as the experiments below demonstrate, even this simple matching strategy can achieve reasonable power.

With the aforementioned matching scheme, we will use the plug-in weights from (
16
) and a projection direction
u=\mathbb{E}_{{v\sim\text{Unif}(S_{d_{X}}^{+})}}\left[{v}\right]
. We estimate the conditional mean function using sample splitting. The dataset is divided into two equal parts: the first split is used to estimate the conditional mean
\mathbb{E}\left[{X}\ \middle|\ {Y,Z}\right]
required for the plug-in weights, and the second split is used to run the
PairSwap-ICI
testing procedure. This separation prevents the weight estimation step from introducing bias in the testing stage. We evaluate performance across sample sizes
n\in\{200,400,600,800,1000,1500,2000\}
,
and repeat each experiment over
1000
Monte Carlo trials.

Figure
4
presents the Type I error curves and power curves of the
PairSwap-ICI
test as functions of the sample size for the three data generating mechanisms. Across all settings, the Type I error curves remain below the pre-determined significance level
\alpha=0.1
, as anticipated by Theorem
13
. Under the alternative
\beta=0.5
, the power increases steadily with the sample size and approaches one as
n\rightarrow\infty
. These results indicate that the proposed method is capable of detecting a variety of conditional dependence structures even when the response variable and the conditioning variables are multivariate.

Figure 4:
Empirical rejection probabilities of the
PairSwap-ICI
test in multivariate settings.
Each column corresponds to one of the three data generating mechanisms, while the curves show results for different covariate dimensions
d_{Z}\in\{2,3\}
.
The first row reports Type I error rates under the null (
\beta=0
), and the second row reports power under the alternative (
\beta=0.5
).

Additional experiments.

In Appendix
E
, we compare the Type I error control and power of the proposed
PairSwap-ICI
test with several well-known benchmark methods for conditional independence testing under the aforementioned simulation settings, namely the Generalized Covariance Measure (GCM), Projected Covariance Measure (PCM), and Conditional Randomization Test (CRT).

7
Experiment on real data: risk factors for diabetes

We now evaluate the performance of our proposed testing procedure on a dataset
5
5

5

The data for this experiment were obtained from
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/34/diabetes
. Additional data descriptions can be found in
Smith et al. (1988)
.
on the incidence of diabetes among the Pima population near Phoenix, Arizona, originally collected by the US National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases. The dataset comprises
768
observations, and includes information on whether each patient has been diagnosed with diabetes according to World Health Organization standards. Additional variables provide data on the number of pregnancies, plasma glucose concentration, diastolic blood pressure, triceps skinfold thickness, 2-hour serum insulin levels, body mass index (BMI), diabetes pedigree function and age.
Recognizing that age is a primary risk factor for diabetes, we examine whether diabetes incidence is independent of any other individual risk factors (e.g., glucose concentration, BMI, insulin level), conditional on age.

It is well-known that the likelihood of developing diabetes increases with age (e.g., the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
6
6

6

For more details, see

https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/risk-factors/index.html
for a list of diabetes risk factors as officially reported by the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.
lists advanced age as one of the risk factors for Type
1
and Type
2
diabetes). Therefore, if we choose
X
to be a binary variable representing incidence of diabetes and
Z
as the age of the patient, then we would expect
X
to exhibit stochastic monotonicity with respect to
Z
(i.e., we expect that Assumption
1
holds, at least approximately).
Most of the other variables, such as BloodPressure, BMI, Glucose, and Pregnancies, are also considered potential risk factors for diabetes—but does this association remain
after we control for age? See Figure
5
for a visualization of the association between diabetes and each of these variables.

Figure 5:
Scatter plots of
X
(jittered for better visibility) and other feature variables along with the fitted logistic regression models to demonstrate the dependence among these variables and
Diabetes Incidence
.

Experiment
1
: marginal independence testing:

We start with a warm-up experiment. We consider six variables:
Pregnancies
,
Glucose
,
BloodPressure
,
SkinThickness
,
Insulin
, and
BMI
, and aim to assess whether each of them is an individual risk factor for diabetes incidence. Specifically, we test the hypothesis
H_{0}:X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y
, where
Y
represents one of the six variables listed above, while
X
is
Diabetes
(and since we are testing marginal rather than conditional independence, we do not attempt to control for
Z
, i.e.,
Age
). For this purpose, we will be using the permutation test for independence with
T(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}
, as outlined in Section
1.2
.

Experiment
2
: conditional independence testing, after controlling for age:

Next, for the same set of six choices for
Y
, we test the hypothesis
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}:X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z
, where
Z
denotes
Age
(and
X
is
Diabetes
as before). This allows us to identify risk factors for diabetes after controlling for age. As noted earlier, we expect the distribution of
X\mid Z=z
to be stochastically monotone in
z
, which supports the application of the
PairSwap-ICI
testing procedure developed in this paper for this purpose. The
PairSwap-ICI
method is implemented with neighbor matching or with cross-bin matching (with
K=50
bins), and our statistic
T
takes the form in (
11
) with linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
and weights
w_{\ell}=\max\{Y_{i_{\ell}}-Y_{j_{\ell}},0\}
.

Experiment
3
: conditional independence testing, with synthetic control
\tilde{X}
:

Finally, we consider a semi-synthetic experiment where
X
is replaced by synthetic observations
\tilde{X}
, generated from an estimated model for
P_{X\mid Z}
that satisfies stochastic monotonicity. We then test the hypothesis
\tilde{X}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y\mid Z
for the same choices of
Y
from Experiment
1
. Since
\tilde{X}
is generated solely based on
Z
and obeys stochastic monotonicity, the null hypothesis
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
holds trivially in this synthetic setting. The validity of our procedure should therefore ensure that the
p
-values generated by
PairSwap-ICI
are (super-)uniformly distributed.

To generate the synthetic feature
\tilde{X}
, since
X
is binary, it suffices to fit an isotonic regression to estimate the conditional mean

\mathbb{E}\left[{X}\ \middle|\ {Z}\right]
and then sample
\tilde{X}
from the Bernoulli distribution with this fitted conditional mean. Following the theory of
Henzi et al. (2021, Theorem 1)
, this is the best approximation to
P_{X\mid Z}
under the
continuous ranked probability score (CRPS)
, while respecting the monotonicity constraint.

-values, averaged over random
3{,}000
subsamples with standard errors in parentheses, from Experiments 2 and 3. Values significant at the
0.05
level are shown in bold.

Results:

For each experiment, we repeat the following procedure
3{,}000
times. We randomly split the full dataset into two halves: a training set and a test set. For Experiment 3, since constructing a synthetic control requires estimating
P_{X\mid Z}
, we use the training half for this preliminary estimation problem (for Experiments 1 and 2 the training set is not used), and the test set to compute the test statistic and the corresponding
p
-value.
We compute
p
-values on the test set using the permutation test for marginal independence and the
PairSwap-ICI
test for conditional independence. Finally, we report the average of the
3{,}000
test-set
p
-values, along with the corresponding choice of the
Y
variable, in Tables
1
and
2
.

Under the marginal independence test, four of the six variables are identified as having significant association with
Diabetes
, but, once we test conditional independence with the
PairSwap-ICI
test, only two of these associations are identified as significant.
Specifically,
Glucose
and
BMI
both are identified as potential risk factors at the
0.05
level of significance by the marginal independence test, and also by the
PairSwap-ICI
test, even after controlling for
Age
.
On the other hand, the variables
Pregnancies
and
Insulin
are significant only under the marginal test; this suggests that, after controlling for
Age
, the data does not provide sufficient evidence to support them as risk factors for
Diabetes
.

Finally, we also note that all the averaged
p
-values from Experiment 3 with synthetic control
\tilde{X}
are concentrated around
0.5
, for each of the choices of
Y
. Since
(\tilde{X},Y,Z)
satisfy
H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
, the
p
-values from Experiment 3 should be roughly uniform (or, if the test is conservative, super-uniform), and thus this behavior is expected from Theorem
2
.

8
Discussion

In this paper, we have developed a nonparametric test of conditional independence assuming only stochastic monotonicity of the conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}
.
This nonparametric constraint is natural in many applications, and allows us to circumvent the impossibility of assumption-free conditional independence testing
(
Shah and Peters, 2020
)
.
We have introduced a variety of approaches to constructing a valid test statistic.
Our test controls the Type I error in finite samples and has power against an array of alternatives. We close with some connections to the literature, and potential avenues for future work.

•

Optimal power in general settings.

In Theorem
12
, we were able to eradicate the earlier gap between the upper and lower bounds on the asymptotic conditional power of our test under the assumption that the conditional distribution of
Y\mid Z
is symmetric, but it remains an open question whether other tests (or, perhaps, the
PairSwap-ICI
but with a different kernel) may be able to avoid this assumption.

•

Alternative methods for finite-sample Type I error control?
While the stochastic monotonicity assumption facilitates consistent estimation of the conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z}
using isotonic distributional regression
(
Mösching and Dümbgen, 2020
;
Henzi et al., 2021
)
, and then applying a conditional independence test which assumes knowledge (or a good estimate) of
P_{X\mid Z}

(
Berrett et al., 2020
;
Candès et al., 2018
)
, these tests will only be valid asymptotically. However, under the same setting, the proposed
PairSwap-ICI
test enjoys finite sample Type I error control without any further restrictions on the choice of matching, or weighting. An interesting open question would be to investigate whether these established approaches for CI testing can also be modified to attain validity in finite samples under the monotonicity assumption.

•

Avoiding data splitting.
The oracle matching test derived in Section
3
requires modelling the conditional mean and conditional variance of the kernel
\psi(X_{i},X_{j})
as a function of
Y_{i},Y_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j}
. We proposed to estimate these moments on a hold-out dataset. Can we instead perform cross-fitting to improve power and retain finite-sample error control?

•

Connection with knockoffs and conditional randomization tests.
Creating synthetic copies of
\mathbf{X}
via pairwise swaps resembles other conditional independence testing procedures, such as knockoffs and the conditional randomization test
(
Candès et al., 2018
)
, and the conditional permutation test
(
Berrett et al., 2020
)
. An advantage of the
PairSwap-ICI
test is that we do not require knowledge of
P_{X|Z}
apart from its stochastic monotonicity, though the potential price is that the test may be more conservative (i.e., the resulting
p
-value may be super-uniform), since we are not working under the “sharp null”.

•

Alternative shape constraints.
We view stochastic monotonicity as a natural form of positive dependence for the joint distribution
(X,Z)
. Are there approaches to test conditional independence under other models of dependence, such as likelihood ratio ordering or total positivity, or under other shape constraints, such as unimodality of
P_{X|Z}

(
Karlin, 1968
;
Shaked and Shanthikumar, 2007
;
Mösching and Dümbgen, 2024
)
?

Availability of Data

The data for this experiment was obtained from
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/34/diabetes
and the codes for reproducing all experiments can be found at
https://github.com/jake-soloff/PairSwap-ICI-Experiments
.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
3

The proof of this result follows the same structure as the proof of Theorem
2
.

Step 1: some deterministic properties of the
p
-value.

Define a function
\hat{p}_{M}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\{\pm 1\}^{L})^{M}\to[0,1]
by

\hat{p}_{M}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)})=\frac{1+\sum_{m=1}^{M}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}^{(m)}})\geq T(\mathbf{x})}\right\}}{1+M}.

As in the proof of Theorem
2
, this function is monotone nonincreasing in each
x_{i_{\ell}}
, and monotone nondecreasing in each
x_{j_{\ell}}
.

Step 2: compare to the sharp null.

Define
\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}
as in the proof of Theorem
2
. Following identical arguments as in that proof, we can verify that, for any fixed
\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}
, it holds that

\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)\preceq_{\rm st}\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)

conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
. Since
\hat{p}_{M}=\hat{p}_{M}(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)})
by construction, we therefore have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}}\right\}.

Marginalizing over the random draw of the swaps,
\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textnormal{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{L})
, we therefore have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

Step 3: validity under the sharp null.

We now need to verify the validity of the Monte Carlo
p
-value, under the sharp null. Unlike the first two steps, for this step the arguments are somewhat different from the proof of Theorem
2
.

First, let
\mathbf{s}^{(0)}
be an additional draw from
\textnormal{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{L})
, sampled independently from all other random variables. Then

(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}\big(\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big),

where
\circ
denotes the elementwise product,
and so

\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)

conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
.
Moreover, by construction of the sharp null data
\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}
,

\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}

holds conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s}^{(0)},\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}
, and therefore

\hat{p}_{M}\Big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}\hat{p}_{M}\Big((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big)

holds conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{s}^{(0)},\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}
. Combining all these calculations so far, then,

\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}\hat{p}_{M}\Big((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big),

(29)

conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
.

Next we calculate this last
p
-value: by definition,

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{M}\Big((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\sum_{m=1}^{M}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(m)}}\bigr)\geq T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\bigr)}\right\}}{1+M}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{m=0}^{M}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(m)}}\bigr)\geq T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\bigr)}\right\}}{1+M},

where the first step holds since, for each
m\in[M]
,

\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\bigr)^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(m)}}=(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(m)}}=(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(m)}}

by definition of the swap operation. In other words,
\hat{p}_{M}\Big((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big)
simply compares the statistic
T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\bigr)
against the
M+1
values
T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}\bigr),\dots,T\bigl((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(M)}}\bigr)
.
We therefore have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\Big((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big)\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{X}_{\sharp},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\alpha,

since, conditional on
\mathbf{X}_{\sharp},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
, the sign vectors
\mathbf{s}^{(0)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}
are independent and identically distributed, and therefore the rank of
T((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}})
among the list
T((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}}),\dots,T((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(M)}})
is uniformly distributed.
Marginalizing over
\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}
, therefore,

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\Big((\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}^{(0)}};\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(0)}\circ\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\Big)\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\alpha.

Finally, combining this with our earlier calculation (
29
), we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\hat{p}_{M}\big(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp};\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{s}^{(M)}\big)\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\alpha,

which completes the proof.
\hfill{\square}

Appendix B
Proof of the results from Section
4

In this section, we prove the results presented in Section
4
. Throughout this appendix, we assume that the statistic
T
admits the form in (
11
) with
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
, and that
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
; the partial ordering
\preceq
for
Z
will simply be the usual ordering
\leq
on
\mathbb{R}
. The organization of this appendix is as follows.

•

We begin in Appendix
B.1
by proving finite sample and asymptotic upper and lower bounds on the conditional power of
PairSwap-ICI
test for any valid matching and weighting scheme, and any statistic
T
of the form (
11
) with a shared linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
.

•

Next, in Appendix
B.3
, we specialize these results to the oracle matching under two cases: one assuming access to oracle knowledge of
\mu
(i.e., Theorem
8
) and another with
\mu
estimated from data (i.e., Theorem
9
).

•

Then, we shift our attention to the partially linear Gaussian models in (
24
). In Appendices
B.4
and
B.5
we prove the asymptotic behavior of conditional power for neighbor matching (Theorem
11
) and for cross-bin matching (Theorem
12
), respectively.

•

In Appendix
B.6
we prove the corollaries and lemmas from Section
4
.

•

In Appendix
B.7
we introduce an oracle matching, namely isotonic median matching, and discuss a key property of the same, which allows us to prove the results in Appendix
B.3
.

•

Finally, in Appendix
B.8
we prove the lemmas used in Appendices
B.1
–
B.5
.

B.1
A general result on power of
PairSwap-ICI
test

Here, we consider any valid matching and weighting scheme, and state finite-sample upper and lower bounds on the conditional power
\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}
of the
PairSwap-ICI
test in Theorem
B.1
. Further, under the asymptotic regime of Section
4.3.1
, we derive asymptotic high-probability upper and lower bounds for the same quantity in Corollary
5
.

Theorem B.1
.

Suppose that the triple
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
satisfies (
17
). Fix any matching
M=\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{L},j_{L})\}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
, and any weight vector

\mathbf{w}\in[0,\infty)^{L}
, which may both depend arbitrarily on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
(but not on
\mathbf{X}
). Assume that
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}>0
and fix any
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Let

\epsilon_{1}:=\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}},\quad\epsilon_{2}:=\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{2}}.

Then, writing
\bar{\Phi}:=1-\Phi
, for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\Phi\Biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\cdot\sqrt{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}}}\Biggr)-(C^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}+\delta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\Phi\Biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{hi},\delta})\cdot\sqrt{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}}}\Biggr)+(C^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}+\delta),

(30)

where we write

\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta}=\alpha-C\delta^{-1}\cdot(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}),\,\alpha_{\rm{hi},\delta}=\alpha+C\delta^{-1}\cdot(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}),

with
C>0
depending only on
\alpha
and on
\mathbb{E}_{{\zeta\sim P_{\zeta}}}\left[{\zeta^{4}}\right]
, and
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\mathbb{E}_{{\zeta\sim P_{\zeta}}}\left[{\zeta^{4}}\right]
.

Proof.

Recall from (
5
) that the
p
-value of the
PairSwap-ICI
test is given by

p=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbf{s}\sim\textnormal{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{L})}}\left\{{T_{\mathbf{s}}\geq T}\right\}=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathbf{s}\sim\textnormal{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{L})}}\left\{{\mathbf{s}^{\top}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X})\geq\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X})}\right\}.

From here, the proof is divided into four key steps.

Step 1: a CLT approximation to the
p
-value.

Conditional on
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
and with
\mathbf{s}\sim\textnormal{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{L})
, we see that

\mathbf{s}^{\top}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X})
is a sum of
L
independent random variables, where the
\ell
th term
s_{\ell}\cdot w_{\ell}\cdot\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}
has mean zero, variance
w_{\ell}^{2}(\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X})^{2}
, and third absolute moment
w_{\ell}^{3}|\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}|^{3}
for
\ell\in[L]
. Therefore, by the Berry–Esseen theorem
(
Shevtsova, 2010
, Theorem 1)
,

\displaystyle\left|p-\bar{\Phi}\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\biggl|\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{s}^{\top}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X})}{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}}\geq x}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}-\bar{\Phi}(x)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq 0.56\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{3}^{3}}{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}^{3}}=:\Gamma_{1},

(31)

as long as
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}>0
.
Thus

{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\bar{\Phi}\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}}\right)<\alpha-\Gamma_{1}}\right\}\leq{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\right\}\leq{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\bar{\Phi}\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}}\right)\leq\alpha+\Gamma_{1}}\right\},

or equivalently (since
\bar{\Phi}
is strictly decreasing),

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}>\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}\geq\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1})}\right\}.

Moreover, when
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}=0
, we have
p=1
, so the above inequality holds for that case as well.

After marginalizing over the distribution of
\mathbf{X}
, then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}>\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}\geq\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

(32)

Step 2: approximating
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}
in (
32
).

Write
V_{n}:=2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}
and define

\Gamma_{2}:=\biggl|\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}-1\biggr|.

Then by (
32
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}>\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}\cdot\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}>\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})+\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})\bigr|\Gamma_{2}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

We can do a similar calculation for the upper bound on the conditional power from (
32
). Together, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}>\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})+\left|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})\right|\Gamma_{2}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}\geq\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1})-\left|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1})\right|\Gamma_{2}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

Now fix
\varepsilon_{\alpha}>0
. Define the event

\displaystyle\Omega_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})+\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1})\bigr|\Gamma_{2}\leq\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\varepsilon_{\alpha})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\bigcap\bigl\{\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1})-\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1})\bigr|\Gamma_{2}\geq\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\varepsilon_{\alpha})\bigr\}.

(33)

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}>\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\varepsilon_{\alpha})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}-\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Omega_{\alpha}^{c}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}\geq\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\varepsilon_{\alpha})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Omega_{\alpha}^{c}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

(34)

Step 3: a CLT approximation to
\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}
in (
32
).

Conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
, we have that
\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}
is a sum of
L
independent terms, where for the
\ell
th term we have

\mathbb{E}\left[{w_{\ell}\cdot\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]=w_{\ell}\cdot\Delta_{\ell}\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}),

and

\mathrm{Var}\left({w_{\ell}\cdot\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)=w_{\ell}^{2}\cdot\mathrm{Var}\left({\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)=w_{\ell}^{2}\cdot 2\sigma_{n}^{2}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\bigl|w_{\ell}\cdot\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}-\mathbb{E}\left[{w_{\ell}\cdot\Delta_{\ell}\mathbf{X}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]\bigr|^{3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{|w_{\ell}\sigma_{n}(\zeta_{i_{\ell}}-\zeta_{j_{\ell}})|^{3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 8\sigma_{n}^{3}w_{\ell}^{3}\mathbb{E}_{{P_{\zeta}}}\left[{|\zeta|^{3}}\right]\leq 8\rho_{4}^{3}\sigma_{n}^{3}w_{\ell}^{3},

where we write
\rho_{4}:=\mathbb{E}_{{P_{\zeta}}}\left[{\zeta^{4}}\right]^{1/4}
.
Therefore, another application of the Berry–Esseen theorem gives

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\biggl|\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\geq x}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle-\Phi\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-x\right)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq 0.56\cdot\frac{8\sigma_{n}^{3}\rho^{3}_{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{3}^{3}}{(\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2})^{3}}\leq 1.59\rho_{4}^{3}\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}.

By rescaling to match the denominator in (
34
), we then have

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Biggl|\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mathbf{X}}{V_{n}^{1/2}}\geq x}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}-\Phi\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\frac{xV_{n}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\right)\Biggr|\leq 1.59\rho_{4}^{3}\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}.

Moreover, by (
34
) we now have

\displaystyle\Phi\biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\varepsilon_{\alpha})\cdot\frac{V_{n}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\biggr)-1.59\rho_{4}^{3}\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Omega_{\alpha}^{c}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt\leq\Phi\biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\varepsilon_{\alpha})\cdot\frac{V_{n}^{1/2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\biggr)+1.59\rho_{4}^{3}\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Omega_{\alpha}^{c}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

Step 4: a concentration result, and completion of the proof.

Finally, we show how to choose
\varepsilon_{\alpha}
to control
\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Omega_{\alpha}^{c}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}
appropriately. We will prove in Lemma
B.6
that the terms
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}
and
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{3}
are likely to be well-behaved—in particular,
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{2}
concentrates near
V_{n}^{1/2}
, while
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{3}
is vanishing. Concretely, Lemma
B.6
will show that for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\bigl\{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{2}\in\bigl[V_{n}(1-\epsilon_{*})\vee 0,V_{n}(1+\epsilon_{*})\bigr]\bigr\}\bigcap\bigl\{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{3}\leq V_{n}\epsilon_{*}^{\prime}\bigr\}\Bigm|\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\Bigr]\geq 1-\delta,

where

\epsilon_{*}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{\delta}}\cdot\biggl(2\rho^{2}_{4}\cdot\epsilon_{1}+2(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{1/3}\biggr),\quad\epsilon_{*}^{\prime}=\frac{(24)^{2/3}\rho_{4}^{2}}{\delta^{2/3}}\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2/3}+4\epsilon_{2}^{2/3}.

Hence, since
(1+\epsilon_{*})^{1/2}-1\leq\epsilon_{*}
and
1-\{(1-\epsilon_{*})\vee 0\}^{1/2}\leq\epsilon_{*}\wedge 1\leq\epsilon_{*}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Gamma_{1}\leq\Gamma_{1,*},\Gamma_{2}\leq\epsilon_{*}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq 1-\delta,

where
\Gamma_{1,*}:=0.56\bigl(\epsilon^{\prime}_{*}/\{(1-\epsilon_{*})\vee 0\}\bigr)^{3/2}
and with the convention that
a/0=\infty
for any
a>0
.
Now, we take

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha}:=

\displaystyle\max\Bigl\{\alpha-\bar{\Phi}\bigl(\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1,*})+\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\Gamma_{1,*})\bigr|\epsilon_{*}\bigr),

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\bar{\Phi}\bigl(\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1,*})-\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\Gamma_{1,*})\bigr|\epsilon_{*}\bigr)-\alpha\Bigr\},

if
\epsilon_{*}<1
, and if
\epsilon_{*}\geq 1
, we take
\varepsilon_{\alpha}=\max(\alpha,1-\alpha)
. Also, here we take the convention that
\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(t)=\infty
if
t\leq 0
and
\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(t)=-\infty
if
t\geq 1
. Then, by construction, with this choice of
\varepsilon_{\alpha}
, the event
\Omega_{\alpha}
defined in (
33
) satisfies
\mathbb{P}\left\{{\Omega_{\alpha}^{c}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\delta
.
Combining both cases, we finally have that

\displaystyle\Phi\Biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha-\varepsilon_{\alpha})\cdot\sqrt{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}}}\Biggr)-1.59\rho_{4}^{3}\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\delta

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\Phi\Biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha+\varepsilon_{\alpha})\cdot\sqrt{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}}}\Biggr)+1.59\rho_{4}^{3}\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}+\delta.

Since by construction,
\varepsilon_{\alpha}\in[0,\max(\alpha,1-\alpha)]
, and by properties of
\bar{\Phi}
and
\bar{\Phi}^{-1}
, there exists
C_{\alpha}>0
, depending only on
\alpha
, such that

\varepsilon_{\alpha}\leq C_{\alpha}(\epsilon_{*}+\epsilon_{*}^{\prime}{}^{3/2}).

Therefore, choosing
C>0
, depending only on
\alpha
and
\rho_{4}
, appropriately,

\varepsilon_{\alpha}\leq C\delta^{-1}\cdot(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}),

as required.
∎

B.1.1
Proof of Theorem
5

We start by noting that by Assumption
A1
,
\epsilon_{1}=\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)
, and by Assumption
A2
,

\epsilon_{2}=\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{2}}\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}\vee\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{2}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\cdot\sqrt{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\biggl\{\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\biggl(1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\biggr)\vee\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\biggr\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})-\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\bigr|\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\left(1-\left|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\right|\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right)-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta}).

Now, recalling the definition of
\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta}
from the statement of Theorem
B.1
, we observe that
\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta}=\alpha-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)
for any fixed
\delta>0
. By Assumption
A2
, if
\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}>\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, then

\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\bigr|\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1),

and otherwise, if
2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\leq\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, then

\bigl|\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\bigr|\cdot\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Combining both cases, since
\bar{\Phi}^{-1}
is continuous, we see that

\displaystyle\Phi\Biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha_{\rm{lo},\delta})\cdot\sqrt{1+\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}}}\Biggr)-(C^{\prime}\epsilon_{1}+\delta)

\displaystyle\geq\Phi\biggl(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\{1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}\bigl(\alpha\!-\!\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\biggr)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)-\delta

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\{1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha)\right)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)-\delta

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\mathbf{w}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}}\right)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)-\delta,

where the last equality follows by Lemma
B.7
.
Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, the desired asymptotic lower bound follows. The asymptotic upper bound follows by a very similar argument.

\square

B.2
Hardness of testing
H_{0}^{\mathrm{ICI}}
(proof of Theorem
7
)

By Proposition
6
, it is enough to prove that

\inf_{Q_{X,Y,Z}\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}}\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}\left(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z}\right)\leq L_{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}.

Now consider any
\tilde{\mu}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
. Similar to (
17
), define
\tilde{Q}_{X,Y,Z}
to be the joint distribution of
(X,Y,Z)
when

X=\tilde{\mu}_{n}(Z)+\sigma_{n}\zeta,\penalty\ \penalty\ (Y,Z)\sim P_{Y,Z},\penalty\ \penalty\ \zeta\sim P_{\zeta}.

Now abusing notation, we will write
P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
to denote the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{X}
given
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, under the joint distribution
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\sim P^{n}_{X,Y,Z}
. Similarly, we will write
Q_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
. Then by properties of Hellinger distance, since the marginal distribution of
(Y,Z)
is identical under
P_{X,Y,Z}
as under
Q_{X,Y,Z}
by construction, we have

\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z})=\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}(P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}},Q_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}})}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{H}(P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}},Q_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}})}\right],

where the last step holds since total variation distance is bounded by Hellinger distance
(
Le Cam and Yang, 2000
)
.
Moreover, by construction, the distribution
P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
has density

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{n}^{-1}f_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\mu_{n}(Y_{i},Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right),

while

Q_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}
has density

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{n}^{-1}f_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{n}(Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right).

Since the squared Hellinger distance is subadditive over product distributions
(
Canonne, 2020
, Appendix C.2)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{2}\left(P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}},Q_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\sqrt{\sigma_{n}^{-1}f_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\mu_{n}(Y_{i},Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right)}-\sqrt{\sigma_{n}^{-1}f_{\zeta}\left(\frac{x_{i}-\tilde{\mu}_{n}(Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right)}\right]^{2}\!\!\!\mathsf{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\sqrt{f_{\zeta}\left(x_{i}-\frac{\mu_{n}(Y_{i},Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right)}-\sqrt{f_{\zeta}\left(x_{i}-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}(Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right)}\right]^{2}\!\!\!\mathsf{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(L_{\zeta}\cdot\left|\frac{\mu_{n}(Y_{i},Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}(Z_{i})}{\sigma_{n}}\right|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(L_{\zeta}\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\right)^{2},

where the second step holds by a change of variables in integration, and the third step holds by the condition (
22
). Returning to our calculations above, then,

\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z})\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{L_{\zeta}\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}\right].

Since we can choose any
\tilde{\mu}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
, then,

\inf_{Q_{X,Y,Z}\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}}\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z})\leq\inf_{\tilde{\mu}_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}}\mathbb{E}\left[{L_{\zeta}\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}\right]=L_{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{ISNR}_{n},

which completes the proof.
\hfill{\square}

B.3
Power of oracle and plug-in matching strategies (proof of Theorems
8
and
9
)

In this section we prove Theorems
8
and
9
using the general upper and lower bounds on the conditional power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test from Theorem
B.1
.

Before turning to the proofs, we recall that by the discussion in Section
4.3.2
, for the oracle matching, we may take
\mathbf{w}^{*}=\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
. When we do not have access to oracle knowledge of
\mu_{n}
, we assume that we are able to learn estimates
\hat{\mu}_{n}
of
\mu_{n}
from an independent dataset, independent of
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
. With these estimates in place, the plug-in weights is simply given by
\hat{\mathbf{w}}=\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
.

B.3.1
Proof of Theorem
8

Given access to oracle knowledge of
\mu_{n}
, we observe that
\hat{\mu}_{n}=\mu_{n}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
9
. Hence, Theorem
8
follows as an immediate corollary of Theorem
9
.
\hfill\Box

B.3.2
Proof of Theorem
9

Our proof is split into three steps. First we state a key property of the oracle matching
M^{\star}
and oracle weight
\mathbf{w}^{\star}
. Then, we use this to deduce similar properties for plug-in matching
\hat{M}
and the plug-in weights
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
. These in turn enable us to show that Assumptions
A1
,
A2
and
A3
of Corollary
5
are satisfied by plug-in matching, and hence the result follows.

Our notation
\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
and
\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
suppresses the dependence of the matching
M
for which it is computed. Since we will need both matchings
M^{\star}
and
\hat{M}
in this step, we make the dependence on the matching explicit by writing
\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)
and
\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)
, for any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
.
Throughout this proof, we write

\displaystyle\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}}{\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}},

\displaystyle\textnormal{Err}_{\infty}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{\infty}^{4}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}}.

Step 1: a key property of oracle matching and its implications.

We start with a key property of oracle matching, which relates
\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M^{\star})\|_{2}
to the isotonic SNR: given
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, we have that

\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\leq\frac{\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M^{\star})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}}=\frac{\sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}}\leq\sqrt{2}\,\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}.

(35)

The equality is simply due to the definition of
M^{*}
as an optimal matching (note that
\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
is a finite set and so the supremum must be attained at some
M^{*}\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
), while the two inequalities follow from Theorem
B.3
, proved later in Appendix
B.7
.

Further, we require a very similar result for the plug-in matching.
To this end, we note that for any
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}-\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}),

(36)

where the final step is from the assumptions of the theorem.
Further, by definition of the plug-in matching in (
16
) and by (
36
), we have that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}\geq\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M^{\star})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M^{\star})\|_{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(\widehat{\sigma_{n}\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n})

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr).

By a similar argument, then, we can establish

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr),

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n},

(37)

i.e., a property similar to (
35
) for plug-in matching with both
\mu_{n}
, and
\hat{\mu}_{n}
.
As a result, we deduce that for plug-in matching,

\displaystyle\max\biggl\{\frac{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}\vee\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{4}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\hat{M})\|_{2}},\,\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n}),\,\textnormal{Err}_{\infty}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})\biggr\}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

(38)

Step 2: establishing Assumptions
A1
and
A2
.

Henceforth we work with the matching
\hat{M}
. We have by (
38
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}}{\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}+\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}-\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\bigl(\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\bigr)+\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})}{1-\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1),

i.e., Assumption
A1
holds for plug-in matching with weights
\hat{\mathbf{w}}=\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
. Moreover, (
38
) also yields that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}+\bigl\|\bigl(\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\!-\!\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)\!\circ\!\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}\cdot\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}+\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}\cdot\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\leq 2\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\cdot\bigl(1+\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})\bigr)=\mathrm{o}_{P}\bigl(\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}\bigr).

Next, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we have that

\displaystyle\bigl|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle-\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\bigl(\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)^{\top}\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\cdot\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\cdot\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\left(1+\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})\right)\cdot\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n}).

Hence, by
(
38
),

\bigl|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}\bigr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}\bigl(\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}\bigr),

(39)

meaning that

\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}={\rm O}_{P}(|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})|).

Combining these calculations verifies that

\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}={\rm o}_{P}(|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})|),

or in other words,

\|\hat{\mathbf{w}}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}={\rm o}_{P}(|\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})|),

and thus the first condition of Assumption
A2
is satisfied.
Next, we focus on the second condition of the same assumption. We start with writing

\displaystyle\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}+\|\bigl(\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}+\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}.

For the first term in the last expression, by (
38
) we have that

\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}=\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{6}^{2}\leq\left(\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{4}\right)^{1/3}\\
\leq\left(\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2}(2\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty})^{4}\right)^{1/3}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}).

For the second term, again by (
38
),

\displaystyle\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2/3}\cdot\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{1/3}\cdot\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\left(\frac{\|\bigl(\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)\|_{2}^{2/3}}{\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2/3}}\times\frac{\|\bigl(\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)\|_{\infty}^{1/3}}{\sigma_{n}^{1/4}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{1/12}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 318.67078pt\left.\times\frac{\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}}{\sigma_{n}^{3/4}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{1/4}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\biggl(\textnormal{Err}_{2}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})^{2/3}\cdot\textnormal{Err}_{\infty}(\hat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})^{1/12}\cdot\frac{\|\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}}{\sigma_{n}^{3/4}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{1/4}}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}).

Finally, we also note that by (
36
) and (
37
),

\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\geq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n})\geq\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}(1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)).

Hence, combining all these calculations,
\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2})
, or in other words,
\|\hat{\mathbf{w}}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\|\hat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{2})
as required. Therefore,
the second condition of Assumption
A2
is also satisfied by plug-in matching with weights
\hat{\mathbf{w}}=\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
.

Step 3: applying Corollary
5
.

Now that we have verified Assumptions
A1
and
A2
, we are ready to apply the corollary.
By (
37
) and (
39
),

\left|\frac{\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\|\hat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{2}}-\|\hat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{2}\right|=\biggl|\frac{\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\|\Delta\hat{\mu}_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}-\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\biggr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}).

Then, by (
35
) and (
37
),

\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)\leq\frac{\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\hat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{2}}\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr).

By Corollary
5
, then,

\displaystyle\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)}{\sqrt{2}}\biggr)-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\bigl(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)\biggr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).\penalty\ \penalty\

Applying Lemma
B.7
completes the proof.
\hfill{\Box}

B.4
Power of neighbor matching (proof of Theorem
11
)

Let us define
\pi
to be any permutation of
[n]
for which

Z_{\pi(1)}\leq Z_{\pi(2)}\leq\cdots\leq Z_{\pi(n)},

and note that the set of matched pairs for neighbor matching is given by
M:=\bigl\{\bigl(\pi(2\ell-1),\pi(2\ell)\bigr)\bigr\}_{\ell\in\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
. By Theorem
5
and Lemma
B.7
, with
\mathbf{w}=\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}
,
it suffices to prove that

\biggl|\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\cdot\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]\Bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\vee 1\Bigr),

(40)

and that Assumptions
A1
and
A2
are satisfied by neighbor matching.

Step 1: establishing (
40
).

Under the partially linear Gaussian model (
24
),

{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})={\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2},

(41)

where the first term is non-positive since
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
. Moreover, we also have
\bigl|{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\bigr|\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{1}
. Since
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
, we also have that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{1}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\bigl(\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(2\ell)})-\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(2\ell-1)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=2}^{n}\bigl(\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(i)})-\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(i-1)})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(n)})-\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(1)})\leq 2\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}.

(42)

Now
\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}\leq 2
, and since
\mu_{0}(Z)
is sub-Gaussian, we have
\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{O}_{P}(\sqrt{\log n})
.
Additionally, by Proposition
B.8
,

\Bigl|\frac{2}{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]\Bigr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

(43)

Moreover,
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]>0
, so it now follows that

\displaystyle\biggl|

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]\Bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl|\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\frac{\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\biggr|+\biggl|\frac{\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]\Bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq 4\,\frac{\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}+\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigr)=\mathrm{O}_{P}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log n}}{\sigma_{n}\sqrt{n}}\right)+\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigr)=\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\vee 1\Bigr),

where in the penultimate line,
since
\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{O}_{P}(\sqrt{\log n})
and
\sigma_{n}\gg\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}
, by (
43
), we have that
\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/(\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2})=\mathrm{O}_{P}\bigl(\sqrt{\log n}/(\sigma_{n}\sqrt{n})\bigr)=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)
.
This establishes (
40
).

Step 2: establishing Assumptions
A1
and
A2
.

Since
Y
is bounded, and
\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}=\mathrm{O}_{P}(\sqrt{n})
by (
43
), Assumption
A1
holds for neighbor matching. Next, turning to Assumption
A2
, we
first observe that by (
42
),

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{1}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}+2\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}.

(44)

Also, by (
40
), it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}+2\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\beta_{n}\sqrt{n/2}\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]\Bigr\}^{1/2}\!-\!\mathrm{o}_{P}(\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{4\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}+\frac{2\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}}{\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})}\right]\Bigr\}^{1/2}\!-\!\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigr)}.

As before,
\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/(\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2})=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)
. Now consider first the case where
\beta_{n}>\sigma_{n}
. Recalling that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]>0
(by assumption), we obtain

\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Otherwise, if
\beta_{n}\leq\sigma_{n}
, then by (
44
),

\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}\leq\frac{4\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}+\frac{2\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Hence, the first condition of Assumption
A2
is satisfied under neighbor matching. For the second condition of Assumption
A2
, we have immediately in the case
\beta_{n}\leq\sigma_{n}
that

\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}\leq\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Finally, in the case
\beta_{n}>\sigma_{n}
, we have
\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}/\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\beta_{n})
. Moreover, by the same argument as in (
44
), and noting that
\|a\|_{4}\geq d^{-1/4}\,\|a\|_{2}
for any
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by Cauchy–Schwarz, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{4}^{2}-\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}n^{-1/2}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}-4\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\bigl(n^{-1/2}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr).

(45)

Next, note that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}\leq\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}^{2}+\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 4\bigl(\beta_{n}+\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 4\beta_{n}\bigl(1+\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\,\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr),

and also that

\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{1/3}\cdot\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2/3}.

Thus, by (
43
) and (
44
), if
\beta_{n}>\sigma_{n}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{1/3}}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4^{1/3}\bigl(1+\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\,\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)^{1/3}}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{1/3}\bigl(n^{-1/2}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr)^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4^{1/3}+4^{1/3}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{1/3}\,\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{1/3}\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}}(Y)\bigr]/\sqrt{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr\}^{1/3}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Therefore, combining both cases, the second condition of Assumption
A2
is also satisfied by neighbor matching. This completes the proof.
\hfill{\square}

B.5
Power of cross-bin matching (proof of Theorem
12
)

In this section, we study the power of the
PairSwap-ICI
test for cross-bin matching.

Definition 2
.

For any probability distribution
P
on
\mathbb{R}
, we define the deviance of
P
as

\textnormal{Dev}(P):=\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-F^{-1}(q)\bigr)^{2}}\right],

(46)

where
F^{-1}
is the quantile function of
P
.

We remark that deviance is a general measure of deviation: in fact, for any distribution,
\textnormal{Dev}(P)\geq 2\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right)
, as we will prove formally in Lemma
D.3
.

With this definition in place, we first state a general concentration result for the conditional power of cross-bin matching in the following theorem.

Theorem B.2
.

Under the same setting and assumptions as the first part of Theorem
12
, the conditional power of cross-bin matching (
Algorithm

2
) satisfies

\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}=\Phi\biggl(\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)+\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{2\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr)^{1/2}\biggr)+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

This result will allow us to immediately verify Theorem
12
(see below), but will also be useful for proving additional results later on.

B.5.1
Proof of Theorem
12

By Lemma
D.3
, for any distribution
P
on
\mathbb{R}
, it holds that
\textnormal{Dev}(P)\geq 2\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}({X})
. Therefore,
\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr)^{1/2}\geq\sqrt{2}\,\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]\bigr)^{1/2}
, and so the first part of Theorem
12
follows as an immediate corollary of Theorem
B.2
.

For the second part, we note that by Lemma
D.3
, for any distribution
P
on
\mathbb{R}
that is symmetric around its mean, it holds that
\textnormal{Dev}(P)=4\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}({X})
. Applying this calculation to
P_{Y|Z}
, then, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]=4\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]
. Thus, the second part follows from Theorem
B.2
.

\hfill{\square}

B.5.2
Proof of Theorem
B.2

The proof for cross-bin matching follows the same key steps as in the proof for neighbor matching in Appendix
B.4
.
We first show that

\biggl|\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\vee 1\Bigr)

(47)

and then establish that Assumptions
A1
and
A2
are satisfied by cross-bin matching. Finally, we will apply Corollary
5
with
\mathbf{w}=\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}
to conclude the proof.

Step 1: establishing (
47
).

Recall the decomposition (
41
). Since
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
, the first term is non-positive, and we also have that
\bigl|{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\bigr|\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{1}
. Next, recalling the notation from
Algorithm

2
, and using the fact that
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\bigl(\mu_{0}(Z_{r_{k+1,s}})-\mu_{0}(Z_{r_{k,m+1-s}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\bigg\rfloor\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\bigl(\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi((k+1)m)})-\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi((k-1)m+1)})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n}{K_{n}}\bigl(\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(n)})-\mu_{0}(Z_{\pi(1)})\bigr)\leq\frac{2n}{K_{n}}\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}.

(48)

Since
\mu_{0}(Z)
is sub-Gaussian,
\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{O}_{P}(\sqrt{\log n})
. Also, since
Y
is bounded and
K_{n}\gg\sqrt{n\log n}/\sigma_{n}
, we have that

\bigl|{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\bigr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\sqrt{n}).

Moreover by Proposition
B.9
,

\frac{2}{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\overset{P}{\to}\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right].

(49)

Therefore, we finally have that

\displaystyle\biggl|

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl|\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\frac{\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\biggr|+\biggl|\frac{\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|

\displaystyle=\biggl|\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}\biggr|+\biggl|\frac{\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}-\frac{\sqrt{n/2}\cdot\beta_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr\}^{1/2}\biggr|

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}\sqrt{n})}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}+\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\Bigr)=\mathrm{o}_{P}\Bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\vee 1\Bigr).

This establishes (
47
).

Step 2: establishing Assumptions
A1
and
A2
.

Since
Y
is bounded, we have by (
49
) that Assumption
A1
holds for the weights
\mathbf{w}=\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}
of cross-bin matching. Next, turning to Assumption
A2
, we
first observe that by (
48
),

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}\|_{\infty}\bigl(\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\bigr)
