\displaystyle\leq 2\bigl(\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{1}^{1/2}\|\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{1/2}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\biggl(\Bigl(\frac{4n}{K_{n}}\Bigr)^{1/2}\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{O}_{P}\biggl(\sqrt{\frac{n\log n}{K_{n}}}+\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}\biggr)=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sqrt{\sigma_{n}}\sqrt[4]{n\log n})+\mathrm{O}_{P}(\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}).

(50)

Hence, in combination with (
47
), it follows that if
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\beta_{n}/\sigma_{n}>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}^{-1/2}(n\log n)^{1/4})+\mathrm{O}_{P}\bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\bigr)}{\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n/2}}{\sigma_{n}}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr\}^{1/2}-\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}\mathrm{o}_{P}\bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{o}_{P}\bigl(\sigma_{n}^{-1/2}(n\log n)^{1/4}\bigr)+\mathrm{O}_{P}\bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\bigr)}{\mathrm{O}_{P}\bigl(\frac{\beta_{n}\,n}{\sigma_{n}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}^{-1/2}n^{-3/4}\log^{1/4}n)+\mathrm{O}_{P}(n^{-1/2})=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1),

since
\sigma_{n}\gg\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]>0
. Otherwise, if
\beta_{n}\ll\sigma_{n}
, then

\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}\bigl(\sigma_{n}^{-1/2}n^{-1/4}\log^{1/4}n\bigr)+\mathrm{O}_{P}(\beta_{n}/\sigma_{n})=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Thus, the first condition of Assumption
A2
is satisfied by cross-bin matching. Moreover, if
\beta_{n}\ll\sigma_{n}
, then

\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}\leq\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Next, suppose
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\beta_{n}/\sigma_{n}>0
. We note that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}+\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\bigl(\|\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}+\beta_{n}\bigr)=\mathrm{O}_{P}(\sqrt{\log n}+\beta_{n}),

and that

\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}\leq\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{1/3}\,\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{2/3}.

Since
\sigma_{n}\gg\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}
, by the same argument as in (
50
), we further have that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{4}^{2}-\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}n^{-1/2}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sqrt{\sigma_{n}}\sqrt[4]{n\log n})

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}n^{-1/2}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}-\mathrm{o}_{P}(\sigma_{n}n^{1/2})

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{n}n^{-1/2}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2}\{1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\}.

Hence, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{\infty}^{1/3}}{\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{O}_{P}\bigl(\log^{1/6}n+\beta_{n}^{1/3}\bigr)}{\beta_{n}^{1/3}n^{-1/6}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}^{2/3}\{1-\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\}^{1/3}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Therefore, the second condition of Assumption
A2
is also satisfied by cross-bin matching.

Step 3: completing the proof

By Corollary
5
, (
47
), and Lemma
B.7
, we have

\displaystyle\biggl|\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}-\Phi\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{2\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr)^{1/2}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha)\biggr)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl|\mathbb{P}\left\{{p\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}-\Phi\biggl(\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha)\biggr)\biggr|

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\biggl|\Phi\biggl(\frac{{\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}}^{\top}\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|_{2}}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha)\biggr)-\Phi\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}}{2\sigma_{n}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y\mid Z})}\right]\bigr)^{1/2}-\bar{\Phi}^{-1}(\alpha)\biggr)\biggr|

\displaystyle=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1),

as required.
\hfill{\square}

B.6
Proof of propositions, corollaries and lemmas from Section
4

B.6.1
Proof of Proposition
6

Fix a distribution
Q_{X,Y,Z}\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
. For any test function

\phi:(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})^{n}\to[0,1]\textnormal{ such that }\sup_{P\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}}\mathbb{E}_{{P}}\left[{\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right]\leq\alpha,

we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{P_{X,Y,Z}}}\left[{\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{{Q_{X,Y,Z}}}\left[{\phi(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right]+\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}\left(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\textnormal{d}_{\textnormal{TV}}\left(P^{n}_{X,Y,Z},Q^{n}_{X,Y,Z}\right).

The result now follows since the last inequality holds for any
Q_{X,Y,Z}\in H_{0}^{\textnormal{ICI}}
.

B.6.2
Proof of Lemma
10

Under the model class (
24
), since
\mathbb{E}\left[{Y}\right]=0
, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}}}\left[{\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\mu_{0}(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}}}\left[{\|\beta_{n}\mathbf{Y}\|_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{\leq}\beta_{n}\biggl(\mathbb{E}_{P_{Y,Z}}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\biggr]\biggr)^{1/2}=\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}\bigl(\mathrm{Var}\left({Y}\right)\bigr)^{1/2},

where the inequality
(1)
holds since
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
, and
(2)
holds by Jensen’s inequality.
Next, again under the model class (
24
), writing
\mathcal{G}
for the set of Borel measurable functions from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}

\displaystyle=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}}\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}}}\left[{\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}\right]\geq\inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}}}\left[{\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}}}\left[{\bigl\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\bigr\|_{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{{P_{Y,Z}}}\left[{\bigl\|\beta_{n}\penalty\ \bigl(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{Y}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\bigr)\bigr\|_{2}}\right].

Finally, since
Y
is a bounded random variable, we have by the weak law of large numbers that

\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{Y}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\bigr\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-E[Y\mid Z_{i}])^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]\,\bigl(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)\bigr).

Thus,
\sigma_{n}\mathrm{ISNR}_{n}\geq\sqrt{n}\beta_{n}\penalty\ \bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]\bigr)^{1/2}\,(1+\mathrm{o}_{P}(1))
.
\hfill{\square}

B.7
An oracle matching: isotonic median matching

A key step in Appendix
B.3.2
is establishing the inequalities in (
35
) for oracle matching, which clarify its relationship to the isotonic SNR,
\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}
. In this section, we will prove these inequalities, restated here in the following theorem:

Theorem B.3
.

For any measurable spaces
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
with
\mathcal{Z}
having a partial order,

\frac{\sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}}\leq\sqrt{2}\,\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}.

Furthermore, when
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
, it holds that

\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}\leq\frac{\sup_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})}\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}}{\sigma_{n}}\leq\sqrt{2}\,\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}.

In order to prove the second part of this result, we will give an explicit construction for an optimal matching, via a technique
that we refer to as isotonic median matching (IMM). In the remainder of this section, therefore we work with
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}
, and we will first develop the definition and properties of the IMM, and will then return to the proof of the theorem.

From this point on, assume that
Z_{1}\leq\cdots\leq Z_{n}
without loss of generality.
Given
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we define the median of this vector by

\textnormal{Med}(a)=\begin{cases}a_{\left(k+1\right)}&\textnormal{if $m=2k+1$ for some $k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$},\\
\frac{a_{(k)}+a_{(k+1)}}{2}&\textnormal{if $m=2k$ for some $k\in\mathbb{N}$,}\end{cases}

where
a_{(1)}\leq\cdots\leq a_{(m)}
denote the order statistics of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
. Given
\mathbf{Z}
, we define the cone of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that obey isotonic relation with
\mathbf{Z}
as

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z}):=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\textnormal{for all }1\leq i\leq j\leq n,\,(z_{i}-z_{j})(v_{i}-v_{j})\geq 0\}.

Moreover, given
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
and any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define
\tilde{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
i
th component given by

\tilde{u}_{i}:=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{i}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr).

(51)

We claim that
\tilde{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z})
—the intuition is that we can think of
\tilde{u}
as a projection of
u
onto the cone
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z})
(in fact, it is the projection with respect to the
\ell_{1}
norm, although we will not establish this claim formally as it is not needed here).
To verify that
\tilde{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z})
, observe that for
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq n
,

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{i_{1}}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{i_{1}}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{i_{1}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{i_{1}}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{i_{2}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{i_{2}}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{i_{2}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)=\tilde{u}_{i_{2}},

which proves the claim.
Next, since
\tilde{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z})
, there exist
m\in[n+1]
, integers
1=n_{1}<\ldots<n_{m}=n+1
and real numbers
r_{1}<\ldots<r_{m-1}
such that

\tilde{u}_{i}=\sum_{t=1}^{m-1}r_{i}\,{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{n_{t}\leq i<n_{t+1}}\right\},

(52)

i.e.,
\tilde{u}
is a piecewise constant vector, with
m-1
components.

The following lemma establishes some additional properties of this vector.

Lemma B.4
.

In the representation (
52
) of the vector
\tilde{u}
, for each
t\in[m-1]
,

r_{t}=\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{n_{t}\leq\ell\leq n_{t+1}-1}\bigr),

(53)

and moreover, for each
i\in\{n_{t},n_{t}+1,\ldots,n_{t+1}-1\}
,

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{i}}\bigr)\geq r_{t}\geq\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{i}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr).

(54)

Proof.

First, since
\tilde{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z})
, and
\tilde{u}_{n_{t}-1}<\tilde{u}_{n_{t}}
for each
t=2,\dots,m-1
, we have

Z_{n_{t}-1}<Z_{n_{t}}
for each
t=2,\dots,m-1
and consequently

n_{t}\leq\ell\leq n_{t+1}-1\ \Longleftrightarrow\ Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}

for each
t\in[m-1]
.
Therefore, establishing (
53
) is equivalent to proving that

r_{t}=\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr),

which is an immediate consequence of the inequalities in (
54
) (applied with
i=n_{t+1}-1
on the left and with
i=n_{t}
on the right). Therefore we now only need to prove these inequalities.

Upper bound on
r_{t}
.

First we will prove that

r_{t}\leq\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{i}}\bigr)

(55)

for all
t\in[m-1]
and all
i\in\{n_{t},\dots,n_{t+1}-1\}
.
By definition, we have

r_{t}=\tilde{u}_{n_{t}}=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t}}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr).

As noted above,
Z_{j}<Z_{n_{t}}
for any
j<n_{t}
.
Therefore we can write

\{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t}}\}=\{j\in[n]:j<n_{t}\}\cup\{j\in[n]:Z_{j}=Z_{n_{t}}\},

and hence,

r_{t}=\max\Big\{\underbrace{\max_{j<n_{t}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)}_{=:r_{t,1}},\ \underbrace{\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)}_{=:r_{t,2}}\Big\},

where we take the convention that the maximum over an empty set is equal to
-\infty
(to handle the case
t=1
, i.e.,
\{j\in[n]:j<n_{1}\}=\emptyset
, so that
r_{t,1}=-\infty
and
r_{t}=r_{t,2}
.
If instead
t\geq 2
, then we claim that
r_{t,1}\leq r_{t,2}
, so that
r_{t}=r_{t,2}
again. Suppose for a contradiction that
r_{t,1}>r_{t,2}
. Let
\hat{j}\in[n_{t}-1]
and
\hat{k}\in[n]
be such that
Z_{\hat{k}}\geq Z_{n_{t}}
and

r_{t,1}=\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)\,\text{ and }\,r_{t,2}=\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\hat{k}}}\bigr).

(56)

Then,
\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\hat{k}}}\bigr)\geq r_{t,1}>\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\hat{k}}}\bigr)
.
Since
\{j:j<n_{t}\}=\{j:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}\}
(due to
Z
being a nondecreasing vector with
Z_{n_{t}-1}<Z_{n_{t}}
as established above), it holds that
Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}<Z_{n_{t}}
and thus, by Lemma
D.4
,

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\hat{k}}}\bigr)\leq\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\bigr).

Now, we also have

r_{t,1}=r_{t}>\tilde{u}_{n_{t}-1}\geq\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)

(57)

Moreover, by noting that
\{k:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}-1}\}=\{k:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}\}\cup\{k:Z_{k}=Z_{n_{t}-1}\}
and by (
56
), it follows that

\displaystyle\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 45.52458pt=\min\Bigl\{\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr),\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\bigr)\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 45.52458pt=\min\Bigl\{r_{t,1},\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\bigr)\Bigr\}=\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\bigr),

where the last step follows by (
57
). Thus, we have that

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\hat{k}}}\bigr)\geq r_{t,1}>\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{\hat{j}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t}-1}}\bigr),

which is a contradiction.
Thus we again have
r_{t}=r_{t,2}
in this case. Therefore,
for all
t\in[m-1]
,

r_{t}=r_{t,2}=\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t}}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)\leq\min_{i\in\{n_{t},\dots,n_{t+1}-1\}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{i}}\bigr).

This establishes the desired upper bound (
55
) on
r_{t}
.

Lower bound on
r_{t}
.

Next we will prove that

r_{t}\geq\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{i}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr)

(58)

for all
t\in[m-1]
and all
i\in\{n_{t},\dots,n_{t+1}-1\}
. This proof is not simply a symmetric version of the proof of the upper bound, since the vector
\tilde{u}
is defined by taking the maximum of the minimum (and not vice versa).

By definition, we have

r_{t}=\tilde{u}_{n_{t+1}-1}=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\min_{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr).

If
t=m-1
then
n_{t+1}-1=n
, and so
\{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}\}=\{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n}\}
. Therefore, for
t=m-1
we have

\displaystyle r_{t}=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\max_{j\in[n]}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\max_{j\in\{n_{t},\dots,n\}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n}}\bigr).

This proves the claim for the case
t=m-1
.

From this point on we assume
t\in[m-2]
.
As noted above,
Z_{k}>Z_{n_{t+1}-1}
for any
k\geq n_{t+1}
.
Therefore we can write

\{k\in[n]:Z_{k}\geq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}\}=\{k\in[n]:k\geq n_{t+1}\}\cup\{k\in[n]:Z_{k}=Z_{n_{t+1}-1}\},

and hence,

r_{t}=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\min\Big\{\underbrace{\min_{k\geq n_{t+1}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)}_{=:r_{t,j,1}},\ \underbrace{\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr)}_{=:r_{t,j,2}}\Big\}.

Fix any
j
with
Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}
, and suppose that
r_{t,j,1}<r_{t,j,2}
. Then

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)<\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr)

for some
k\geq n_{t+1}
. Then by Lemma
D.4
, we must have

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)\geq\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t+1}-1}<Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr).

Now let
t^{\prime}\geq t+1
be such that
n_{t^{\prime}}\leq k<n_{t^{\prime}+1}
. Since
Z_{n_{t+1}-1}<Z_{n_{t+1}}
as explained above, we have by Lemma
D.4
that

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t+1}-1}<Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)=\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t+1}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)\\
\geq\min\left\{\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t+1}}\leq Z_{\ell}<Z_{n_{t+2}}}\bigr),\dots,\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t^{\prime}-1}}\leq Z_{\ell}<Z_{n_{t^{\prime}}}}\bigr),\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t^{\prime}}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{k}}\bigr)\right\}\\
\geq\min\{r_{t+1},\dots,r_{m-1}\}=r_{t+1},

where the second inequality applies the upper bound (
55
) on each
r_{t+1},\dots,r_{m-1}
. But we also have

r_{t}\geq\min\{r_{t,j,1},r_{t,j,2}\}=r_{t,j,1}\geq r_{t+1},

which is a contradiction.

Therefore we have
r_{t,j,1}\geq r_{t,j,2}
for all
j
such that
Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}
, and so

r_{t}=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}r_{t,j,2}=\max_{j\in[n]:Z_{j}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr)\\
\geq\max_{j\in\{n_{t},\dots,n_{t+1}-1\}}\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{j}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{n_{t+1}-1}}\bigr).

This proves the lower bound (
58
), and thus completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now in a position to define the isotonic median matching for any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
m
be as defined in (
52
). For
t\in[m-1]
, define

\displaystyle P_{t}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\ell\in\{n_{t},n_{t}+1,\ldots,n_{t+1}-1\}:u_{\ell}>r_{t}=\tilde{u}_{\ell}\bigr\},

\displaystyle N_{t}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\ell\in\{n_{t},n_{t}+1,\ldots,n_{t+1}-1\}:u_{\ell}<r_{t}=\tilde{u}_{\ell}\bigr\}.

Let us order the indices in
P_{t}
as
\ell_{t,1}^{+}<\cdots<\ell_{t,n_{t}^{+}}^{+}
and the indices in
N_{i}
as
\ell_{t,1}^{-}<\cdots<\ell_{t,n_{t}^{-}}^{-}
. Defining
\bar{n}_{t}:=n_{t}^{+}\wedge n_{t}^{-}
, we consider the collection of ordered pairs

\mathcal{C}_{t}:=\bigl\{(\ell_{t,1}^{+},\ell_{t,1}^{-}),\cdots,(\ell_{t,\bar{n}_{t}}^{+},\ell_{t,\bar{n}_{t}}^{-})\bigr\}.

Finally, we consider

\widetilde{M}(u):=\bigcup_{t=1}^{m-1}\mathcal{C}_{t}
and call it the isotonic median matching (IMM). In the lemma below, we prove that this is a valid matching.

Lemma B.5
.

Suppose that the elements of
\{u_{\ell}:\ell\in[n]\}
are all distinct. Then for any
t\in[m-1]
, we have
n_{t}^{+}=n_{t}^{-}
and

u_{\ell}=\tilde{u}_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\{n_{t},n_{t}+1,\ldots,n_{t+1}-1\}\setminus\bigl(P_{t}\cup N_{t}\bigr)
.
Moreover,
\widetilde{M}(u)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in[m-1]
. When
n_{t+1}-n_{t}
is even, we have by (
53
) (in Lemma
B.4
) that
n_{t}^{+}=n_{t}^{-}=(n_{t+1}-n_{t})/2
and
\{n_{t},n_{t}+1,\ldots,n_{t+1}-1\}\setminus\bigl(P_{t}\cup N_{t}\bigr)=\emptyset
. On the other hand, when
n_{t+1}-n_{t}
is odd, we have
n_{t}^{+}=n_{t}^{-}=(n_{t+1}-n_{t}-1)/2
and
\{n_{t},n_{t}+1,\ldots,n_{t+1}-1\}\setminus\bigl(P_{t}\cup N_{t}\bigr)=:\{\ell_{0}\}

is a singleton set. Moreover,
u(\ell_{0})=\tilde{u}(\ell_{0})
which proves the first two claims.

In order to prove that
\widetilde{M}(u)
is a valid matching, we need to show that
Z_{\ell_{t,j}^{+}}\leq Z_{\ell_{t,j}^{-}}
for any
t\in[m-1]
and
j\in[\bar{n}_{t}]
. To see this, suppose for a contradiction that
Z_{\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{+}}>Z_{\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{-}}
for some
t\in[m-1]
and some minimal
j_{0}\in[\bar{n}_{t}]
. Since
\tilde{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{ISO}}(\mathbf{Z})
, by (
52
), we have
\ell\geq n_{t}
whenever
Z_{\ell}\geq Z_{n_{t}}
.
Consequently, this gives us

\displaystyle\bigl|\{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{-}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ u_{\ell}<r_{t}\}\bigr|

\displaystyle\geq\bigl|\{\ell:n_{t}\leq\ell\leq\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{-}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ u_{\ell}<r_{t}\}\bigr|=j_{0}

\displaystyle\bigl|\{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{-}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ u_{\ell}>r_{t}\}\bigr|

\displaystyle<\bigl|\{\ell:n_{t}\leq\ell\leq\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{+}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ u_{\ell}>r_{t}\}\bigr|=j_{0},

where the second inequality follows by recalling that
\ell_{t,1}^{+}<\cdots<\ell_{t,n_{t}^{+}}^{+}
.
Since the elements of
\{u_{\ell}:\ell\in[n]\}
are all distinct, this further implies that

\textnormal{Med}\bigl((u_{\ell})_{\ell:Z_{n_{t}}\leq Z_{\ell}\leq Z_{\ell_{t,j_{0}}^{-}}}\bigr)<r_{t},

which contradicts the result (
54
) proved in Lemma
B.4
.
∎

With the definition and properties of the IMM now in place, we are now ready to prove Theorem
B.3
. This result establishes the claim (
35
) in Appendix
B.3.2
, which was used for proving the power guarantees for both oracle and plug-in matching in Theorems
8
and
9
.

Proof of Theorem
B.3
.

We start by proving the upper bound. Fix any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
.
By definition,
\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}}\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}
.
We claim that

\Bigl(\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\Bigr)_{+}\leq\bigl|\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-g\bigl(Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|+\bigl|g\bigl(Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|

(59)

for every
\ell\in[L]
and every
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
. To see this, we may assume that
\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)>\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)
since otherwise the left-hand side is zero, and the inequality trivially holds. Then

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\bigl(\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr)_{+}=\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-g(Z_{i_{\ell}})+g(Z_{j_{\ell}})-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)+g(Z_{i_{\ell}})-g(Z_{j_{\ell}})

\displaystyle\leq\bigl|\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-g\bigl(Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|+\bigl|g\bigl(Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|+\left(g(Z_{i_{\ell}})-g(Z_{j_{\ell}})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\bigl|\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-g\bigl(Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|+\bigl|g\bigl(Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|,

where the last step holds since we must have
Z_{i_{\ell}}\leq Z_{j_{\ell}}
(since
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
) and therefore
g(Z_{j_{\ell}})-g(Z_{j_{\ell}})\leq 0
(since
g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{ISO}}
).
This proves the claim (
59
), and therefore, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\Bigl(\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\Bigr)_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\left(\bigl|\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-g\bigl(Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|+\bigl|g\bigl(Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)-\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bigl\{\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{i_{\ell}},Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)-g\bigl(Z_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\}^{2}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\bigl\{\mu_{n}\bigl(Y_{j_{\ell}},Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)-g\bigl(Z_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)\bigr\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{\mu_{n}(Y_{i},Z_{i})-g(Z_{i})\bigr\}^{2}=2\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\leq 2\sigma_{n}^{2}\,\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}^{2}.

This completes the proof of the first part.

Next we turn to the lower bound. It is sufficient to show that, for any
\epsilon>0
, we can find some
M\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
such that

\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};M)\|_{2}^{2}\geq\sigma_{n}^{2}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}^{2}-\epsilon
.
Fixing any
\epsilon>0
, first we note that we can find some
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that the coordinates of
u
are all distinct and
\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-u\|_{2}<\epsilon/(\sqrt{2}+1)
. Consider the IMM for this vector
u
,
\widetilde{M}(u):=\{(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in\tilde{L}}
, where
\widetilde{M}(u)\in\mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbf{Z})
by Lemma
B.5
.
Furthermore by Lemma
B.5
and (
52
), we have for any
\ell\in[\tilde{L}]
that

u_{i_{\ell}}>\tilde{u}_{i_{\ell}}=\tilde{u}_{j_{\ell}}>u_{j_{\ell}}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\|\Delta u^{+}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\tilde{L}}\bigl(u_{i_{\ell}}-u_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\tilde{L}}\bigl(u_{i_{\ell}}-\tilde{u}_{i_{\ell}}+\tilde{u}_{j_{\ell}}-u_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\ell=1}^{\tilde{L}}\bigl\{\bigl(u_{i_{\ell}}-\tilde{u}_{i_{\ell}}\bigr)^{2}+\bigl(\tilde{u}_{j_{\ell}}-u_{j_{\ell}}\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(u_{i}-\tilde{u}_{i}\bigr)^{2}=\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{2}^{2},

where the penultimate equality holds because
u_{i}=\tilde{u}_{i}
for
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{1},j_{1},\ldots,i_{\tilde{L}},j_{\tilde{L}}\}
, by Lemma
B.5
.
Next, we observe that

\displaystyle\|\Delta u^{+}\|_{2}-\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\widetilde{M}(u))\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta u^{+}-\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\widetilde{M}(u))\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Delta u-\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\widetilde{M}(u))\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\|u-\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}+1},

and that by (
20
),

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{u}\|_{2}\leq\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}+1}.

Therefore,

\|\Delta\mu_{n}^{+}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z};\widetilde{M}(u))\|_{2}\geq\|\Delta u^{+}\|_{2}-\frac{\sqrt{2}\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}+1}\geq\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{2}-\frac{\sqrt{2}\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}+1}\\
\geq\left(\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}-\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}+1}\right)-\frac{\sqrt{2}\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}+1}=\sigma_{n}\widehat{\mathrm{ISNR}}_{n}-\epsilon,

which completes the proof of the second part.
∎

B.8
Proof of lemmas from Appendices
B.1
—
B.5

B.8.1
Lemmas used to prove general power guarantees in Appendix
B.1

Lemma B.6
.

Under the setting and assumptions of Theorem
B.1
,
for any
\delta>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{2}}{\left(2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)}

\displaystyle\in\bigl[(1-\epsilon_{*})\vee 0,1+\epsilon_{*}\bigr]\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\cdot\epsilon_{*}^{\prime}\biggm|\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\biggr\}\geq 1-\delta

where

\epsilon_{*}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{\delta}}\cdot\biggl(2\rho^{2}_{4}\epsilon_{1}+2(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{1/3}\biggr),\quad\epsilon_{*}^{\prime}=\frac{(24)^{2/3}\rho_{4}^{2}}{\delta^{2/3}}\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2/3}+4\epsilon_{2}^{2/3},

and we write
\rho_{4}:=\mathbb{E}_{{P_{\zeta}}}\left[{\zeta^{4}}\right]^{1/4}
.

Proof.

Recall the notation from the hypothesis in statement of Theorem
B.1
:

\epsilon_{1}=\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}}{\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}},\quad\epsilon_{2}=\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{2}}.

Under the model class (
17
),
\Delta\mathbf{X}=\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})+\sigma_{n}\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
, and thus, we can write

\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{2}=\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|^{2}_{2}+2\sigma_{n}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})^{\top}\bigl(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}.

(60)

For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|^{2}_{2}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle=2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left({\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|^{2}_{2}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{4}\sigma_{n}^{4}\mathbb{E}_{{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{\zeta}}}\left[{|\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}|^{4}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 16\rho^{4}_{4}\sigma_{n}^{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{4}_{4}\leq 16\rho^{4}_{4}\sigma_{n}^{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{\infty}\leq 16\rho^{4}_{4}\sigma_{n}^{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{4}_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2},

and therefore by Chebyshev’s inequality, for every
A>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\left|\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|^{2}_{2}-2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\right|\geq A}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\frac{16\rho^{4}_{4}\sigma_{n}^{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{4}_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{A^{2}}.

For the second term in (
60
), we have that

\mathbb{E}\left[{2\sigma_{n}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})^{\top}\bigl(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]=0

and moreover, by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left({2\sigma_{n}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})^{\top}\bigl(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=4\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathrm{Var}\left({\Delta_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right)\cdot\bigl(w_{\ell}\Delta_{\ell}\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=8\sigma_{n}^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{2}\bigl(w_{\ell}\Delta_{\ell}\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq 8\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{6}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}))\|_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq 8\sigma_{n}^{2}\left(\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{4}_{\infty}\right)^{1/3}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=8\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\left(\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{2}\right)^{2/3}\cdot(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{2/3}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq 8\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\cdot(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{2/3},

where the penultimate step uses the definitions of
\epsilon_{1}^{2}
and
\epsilon_{2}
. Another application of Chebyshev’s inequality then gives us for every
B>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\left|2\sigma_{n}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})^{\top}(\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}))\right|\geq B}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\leq\frac{8\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\cdot(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{2/3}}{B^{2}}.

Together, then,

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\left|\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{2}-\left(2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\right|\geq A+B}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\\
\leq\frac{16\sigma_{n}^{4}\rho^{4}_{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{4}_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{A^{2}}+\frac{8\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\cdot(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{2/3}}{B^{2}}.

Next, we choose

\displaystyle A^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{\delta}\cdot 16\rho^{4}_{4}\sigma_{n}^{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{4}_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}^{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}

\displaystyle B^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{\delta}\cdot 8\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\left(\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\cdot(\epsilon_{1}^{2}\epsilon_{2})^{2/3}.

Then

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\frac{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{2}}{\left(2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)}\in[(1-\epsilon_{*})\vee 0,1+\epsilon_{*}]}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\\
\geq 1-\frac{2\delta}{3}.

Next we bound
\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{3}_{3}
.
We start by writing

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{3}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{3}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{3}+\left(\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{3}_{2}\right)^{1/3}\cdot\epsilon_{2}^{1/3}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{3}+\left(\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\right)\cdot\epsilon_{2}^{1/3}.

Further, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{3}^{3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{\ell}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[{|\Delta_{\ell}{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}|^{3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{3}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{\zeta}}}\left[{|\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}|^{3}}\right]\leq\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{3}\cdot 8\rho^{3}_{3}

where
\rho_{3}=\mathbb{E}_{{\zeta\sim P_{\zeta}}}\left[{|\zeta|^{3}}\right]^{1/3}
.
Since
\rho_{3}\leq\rho_{4}
, we further have that

\mathbb{E}\left[{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{3}^{3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]\leq 8\rho_{4}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\infty}\leq 8\rho_{4}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}.

By Markov’s inequality, then,

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\sigma_{n}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{3}^{3}\leq\frac{24\sigma_{n}^{3}\rho_{4}^{3}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{3}_{2}\cdot\epsilon_{1}}{\delta}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{3}.

Together, then, we have

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|_{3}\leq\frac{\sqrt[3]{24}\sigma_{n}\rho_{4}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}\epsilon_{1}^{1/3}}{\delta^{1/3}}+\bigl(\sigma_{n}\|\mathbf{w}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|_{2}\bigr)\epsilon_{2}^{1/3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{3},

which we can relax to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{3}\leq\frac{2(24)^{2/3}\sigma_{n}^{2}\rho_{4}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}\epsilon_{1}^{2/3}}{\delta^{2/3}}+4\bigl(\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\bigr)\epsilon_{2}^{2/3}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 412.56496pt\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{3}.

Thus

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mathbf{X}\|^{2}_{3}\leq\left(2\sigma_{n}^{2}\|\mathbf{w}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mathbf{w}\circ\Delta\mu_{n}(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\|^{2}_{2}\right)\cdot\epsilon_{*}^{\prime}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{3}

as required.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

Consider any real-valued sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\epsilon_{n}\overset{P}{\to}0
and
\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}\overset{P}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
, then

\bigl|\Phi\bigl(x_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})+y+\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}\bigr)-\Phi(x_{n}+y)\bigr|\overset{P}{\to}0.

Proof.

First, since
\Phi
is
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\pi}
-Lipschitz, we have that

\displaystyle\bigl|\Phi\bigl(x_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})+y+\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}\bigr)-\Phi\bigl((x_{n}+y)\cdot(1+\epsilon_{n})\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,|\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}-y\epsilon_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\epsilon_{n}^{\prime}|+|y||\epsilon_{n}|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Hence, it suffices to show that

\bigl|\Phi\bigl((x_{n}+y)\cdot(1+\epsilon_{n})\bigr)-\Phi(x_{n}+y)\bigr|\overset{P}{\to}0.

In fact, without loss of generality, we may take
y=0
.
By the mean value theorem, there exists a real-valued sequence of
(\bar{x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\bar{x}_{n}
lies between
x_{n}
and
x_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})
, such that

\bigl|\Phi\bigl(x_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})\bigr)-\Phi(x_{n})\bigr|=\phi(\bar{x}_{n})\cdot|x_{n}\epsilon_{n}|=\phi\bigl(|\bar{x}_{n}|\bigr)\,|x_{n}|\,|\epsilon_{n}|.

Define the event
A_{n}:=\bigl\{|\bar{x}_{n}|\geq|x_{n}|/2\bigr\}

and note that
\mathbb{P}(A_{n})\geq\mathbb{P}(|\epsilon_{n}|\leq 1/2)
. Since
\epsilon_{n}\overset{P}{\to}0
as
n\to\infty
, we have
\mathbb{P}(A_{n})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, writing
K:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|x|\exp(-x^{2}/8)\in(0,\infty)
, we have on
A_{n}
that,

\phi(|\bar{x}_{n}|)\cdot|x_{n}|\leq\exp\bigl(-x_{n}^{2}/8\bigr)\cdot|x_{n}|\leq K.

Finally fix
\delta>0
and observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl\{\bigl|\Phi\bigl(x_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})\bigr)-\Phi(x_{n})\bigr|\geq\delta\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigl[\bigl\{\bigl|\Phi\bigl(x_{n}(1+\epsilon_{n})\bigr)-\Phi(x_{n})\bigr|\geq\delta\bigr\}\cap A_{n}\Bigr]+\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl[\bigl\{|\epsilon_{n}|\geq\delta/K\bigr\}\cap A_{n}\bigr]+\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\epsilon_{n}|\geq\delta/K\bigr)+\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})\to\,0,

as
n\to\infty
. Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, the result follows.
∎

B.8.2
Concentration results for neighbor matching

We now prove a concentration result for the neighbor matching in the asymptotic regime from Section
4.4
.
Let
(Y_{1},Z_{1}),(Y_{2},Z_{2}),\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Y,Z}
, where
P_{(Y,Z)}
is a distribution on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}
, and let
Z_{(n,1)}\leq\dots\leq Z_{(n,n)}
be the order statistics of
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}
. Abusing notation, we will write
Y_{(n,i)}
to denote the
Y
value corresponding to
Z_{(n,i)}
—that is,

(Y_{(n,1)},\dots,Y_{(n,n)})\mid(Z_{(n,1)},\dots,Z_{(n,n)})\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,1)})\times\dots\times P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,n)}).

Then for neighbor matching at sample size
n
, we are interested in the quantity

{\rm NM}_{n}:=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(Y_{(n,2i-1)}-Y_{(n,2i)})^{2}_{+}.

In the following result, we show that this quantity concentrates around the mean of the conditional variance of
P_{Y|Z}
:

Proposition B.8
.

Under the assumptions and definitions stated above,

\bigl|{\rm NM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]\bigr|={\rm o}_{P}(1).

Proof.

The proof is split into four key steps.

Step 1: a concentration step.

First, we note that conditional on
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}
, the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(Y_{(n,2i-1)}-Y_{(n,2i)})^{2}_{+}
is the sum of
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
independent terms, with each summand lying in
[0,4]
. Hence, for any
\delta>0
, by Hoeffding’s inequality we have for
n\geq 2
that

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\bigl|{\rm NM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm NM}_{n}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]\bigr|\geq 4\cdot\frac{2}{n}\sqrt{\frac{\lfloor n/2\rfloor\cdot\log(2/\delta)}{2}}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right\}\leq\delta.

In particular,

\bigl|{\rm NM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm NM}_{n}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]\bigr|={\rm O}_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

(61)

Step 2: comparing to i.i.d. pairs.

Since
(y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto(y_{1}-y_{2})^{2}_{+}
is
4
-Lipschitz with respect to the
\ell_{1}
norm on
[-1,1]^{2}
, for any two distributions
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
on
[-1,1]^{2}
we have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{{(Y,Y^{\prime})\sim Q}}\left[{(Y-Y^{\prime})^{2}_{+}}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{{(Y,Y^{\prime})\sim Q^{\prime}}}\left[{(Y-Y^{\prime})^{2}_{+}}\right]\right|\leq 4{\rm d}_{\rm W}(Q,Q^{\prime}),

where
{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the
L_{1}
-Wasserstein distance between distributions, defined as

\mathrm{d}_{\rm W}(P,Q):=\inf_{(U,V)\sim(P,Q)}\mathbb{E}\|U-V\|_{2},

where the infimum is taken over all couplings of
U
and
V
with
U\sim P
and
V\sim Q
. In particular, in the special case that
Q=Q_{1}\times Q_{2}
and
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}^{\prime}\times Q_{2}^{\prime}
are both product distributions, then we have

\left|\mathbb{E}_{{(Y,Y^{\prime})\sim Q_{1}\times Q_{2}}}\left[{(Y-Y^{\prime})^{2}_{+}}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{{(Y,Y^{\prime})\sim Q^{\prime}_{1}\times Q^{\prime}_{2}}}\left[{(Y-Y^{\prime})^{2}_{+}}\right]\right|\leq 4\bigl({\rm d}_{\rm W}(Q_{1},Q^{\prime}_{1})+{\rm d}_{\rm W}(Q_{2},Q^{\prime}_{2})\bigr).

Now, fix any
i\in\{1,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor\}
and observe that

\big(Y_{(n,2i-1)},Y_{(n,2i)}\big)\mid(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,2i-1)})\times P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,2i)}).

Hence, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y_{(n,2i-1)}-Y_{(n,2i)}\right)^{2}_{+}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]-\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid z)}(Y)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y_{(n,2i-1)}-Y_{(n,2i)}\right)^{2}_{+}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid z)}}\left[{(Y-Y^{\prime})^{2}_{+}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\bigl\{{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,2i-1)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid z)\right)+{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,2i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid z)\right)\bigr\}.

Now let
Z^{\prime}_{1},Z^{\prime}_{2},\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Z}
be drawn independently of the data. Then, applying the calculations above with
z=Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y_{(n,2i-1)}-Y_{(n,2i)}\right)^{2}_{+}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]-\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})}(Y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\bigl\{{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,2i-1)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})\right)+{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,2i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})\right)\bigr\}.

For
n\geq 3
, summing the inequality above over
i=1,\dots,\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, we obtain

\displaystyle\biggl|\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm NM}_{n}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]-\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})}(Y)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\left(Y_{(n,2i-1)}-Y_{(n,2i)}\right)^{2}_{+}}\ \middle|\ {Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}}\right]-\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})}(Y)\right|+\frac{4}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{n-2}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{j=2i-1}^{2i}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\big(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,j)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})\big)+\frac{4}{n}.

(62)

Step 3: concentration around the mean of conditional variance.

Note that

\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})}(Y)=\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{i})}(Y),

since by definition,
Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,1)},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\lfloor n/2\rfloor)}
is simply a permutation of
Z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
.
Moreover, since
Z^{\prime}_{1},Z^{\prime}_{2},\ldots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Z}
, the random variables
\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{i})}(Y)
are independent and identically distributed, with mean
\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Var}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]
and variance at most 1. Therefore, by the central limit theorem, we have

\biggl|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\textnormal{Var}_{Y\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{i})}(Y)-\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}_{{Y\sim P_{Y\mid Z}}}({Y})\bigr]\biggr|={\rm O}_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

Combining this with (
61
) and (
62
), we have

\left|{\rm NM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Var}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]\right|\\
\leq\frac{8}{n-2}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{j=2i-1}^{2i}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\big(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,j)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})\big)+{\rm O}_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

Step 4: the Wasserstein distance.

Finally, by Lemma
D.5
(applied with
K_{n}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, and
B_{n,k}=\{2k-1,2k\}
for
k=1,\dots,K_{n}
), we have

\frac{8}{n-2}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{j=2i-1}^{2i}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\big(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,j)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor,i)})\big)={\rm o}_{P}(1).

This completes the proof.
∎

B.8.3
Concentration results for cross-bin matching

The aim of this subsection is to prove a concentration result for cross-bin matching under the asymptotic framework of Section
4.4
, analogous to Proposition
B.8
for neighbor matching in Section
B.8.2
. To state and prove the result, we first introduce some more notation for cross-bin matching at sample size
n
with
K_{n}
bins. Let
m_{n}=\lfloor n/K_{n}\rfloor
, and let

A_{n,1}=\big\{1,\dots,m_{n}\big\},\dots,A_{n,K_{n}}=\big\{(K_{n}-1)m_{n}+1,\dots,K_{n}m_{n}\big\}

be an uniform partition of
\{1,\ldots,K_{n}m_{n}\}
into
K_{n}
bins. Given
y,y^{\prime}\in[-1,1]^{m_{n}}
, let
y_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq y_{(m_{n})}
and
y^{\prime}_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq y^{\prime}_{(m_{n})}
denote the sorted values of
y
and
y^{\prime}
, and define
f_{n}:[-1,1]^{m_{n}}\times[-1,1]^{m_{n}}\to[0,\infty)
by

f_{n}(y,y^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}\big(y_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-y^{\prime}_{(i)}\big)^{2}_{+}.

(63)

Building on the notation defined at the beginning of Section
B.8.2
, for any
k\in[K_{n}]
we define

Y_{(n,A_{n,k})}:=(Y_{(n,(k-1)m_{n}+1)},\ldots,Y_{(n,km_{n})})
.
Our quantity of interest can then be written as

{\rm CBM}_{n}:=\frac{2}{n}\|\Delta\mathbf{Y}^{+}\|^{2}_{2}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}f_{n}(Y_{(n,A_{n,k})},Y_{(n,A_{n,k+1})}).

Similar to neighbor matching, the following proposition establishes concentration of the aforementioned quantity around the mean conditional deviance:

Proposition B.9
.

Under the assumptions and definitions stated above, if
K_{n}\to\infty
and
n/K_{n}\to\infty
, then

\left|{\rm CBM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]\right|={\rm o}_{P}(1).

Proof of Proposition
B.9
.

Similar to the concentration result for neighbor matching, this proof is also split into four key steps.

Step 1: a concentration step.

Our first step is a concentration result: we claim that for any
\delta\in(0,1]
, we have

\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\biggl|{\rm CBM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm CBM}_{n}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\biggr|\geq\sqrt{\frac{4\log(4/\delta)}{K_{n}}}\biggm|\mathbf{Z}\biggr\}\leq\delta.

(64)

To prove this, we start by writing

{\rm CBM}_{n}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor K_{n}/2\rfloor}f_{n}(Y_{(n,A_{n,2k-1})},Y_{(n,A_{n,2k})})+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor(K_{n}-1)/2\rfloor}f_{n}(Y_{(n,A_{n,2k})},Y_{(n,A_{n,2k+1})}).

After conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
, the first term is a sum of
\lfloor K_{n}/2\rfloor
independent terms because the sets
\{A_{n,k}:k\in[K_{n}]\}
are pairwise disjoint. Furthermore, each term
2f_{n}(Y_{(n,A_{n,2k-1})},Y_{(n,A_{n,2k})})/n
takes values in
[0,8\cdot\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor/n]\subseteq[0,4/K_{n}]
. Therefore by Hoeffding’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Biggl\{\biggl|

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor K_{n}/2\rfloor}f_{n}(Y_{(n,A_{n,2k-1})},Y_{(n,A_{n,2k})})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor K_{n}/2\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(Y_{(n,A_{n,2k-1})},Y_{(n,A_{n,2k})})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\biggr|\geq\frac{4}{K_{n}}\sqrt{\frac{\lfloor K_{n}/2\rfloor\cdot\log(4/\delta)}{2}}\Biggm|\mathbf{Z}\Biggr\}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}.

We can prove a similar concentration for the second term, and together with a union bound this establishes (
64
). It follows that

\bigl|{\rm CBM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm CBM}_{n}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\bigr|={\rm O}_{P}(K_{n}^{-1/2}).

Step 2: comparing to i.i.d. terms.

Since
(y,y^{\prime})\mapsto f_{n}(y,y^{\prime})
is
4
-Lipschitz with respect to the
\ell_{1}
norm on
[-1,1]^{m_{n}}\times[-1,1]^{m_{n}}
(see Lemma
D.7
), we have for any distributions
Q,Q^{\prime}
on
[-1,1]^{m_{n}}\times[-1,1]^{m_{n}}
that

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{{(Y,Y^{\prime})\sim Q}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})\sim Q^{\prime}}\bigl[f_{n}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})\bigr]\bigr|\leq 4{\rm d}_{\rm W}(Q,Q^{\prime}),

where
{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the
L_{1}
-Wasserstein distance between distributions.
In particular, in the special case where
Q=Q_{1}\times\dots\times Q_{2m_{n}}
and
Q^{\prime}=Q_{1}^{\prime}\times\dots\times Q_{2m_{n}}^{\prime}
, we have

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{{(Y,Y^{\prime})\sim Q}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})\sim Q^{\prime}}\bigl[f_{n}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})\bigr]\bigr|\leq 4\sum_{j=1}^{2m_{n}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}(Q_{j},Q^{\prime}_{j}).

Next, fix any
k\in[K_{n}-1]
. By construction, we have

\big(Y_{(n,A_{n,k})},Y_{(n,A_{n,k+1})}\big)\mid\mathbf{Z}\sim P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,A_{n,k})})\times P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,A_{n,k+1})}),

where, abusing notation, we write

P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,A_{n,k})})=P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,(k-1)m_{n}+1)})\times\dots\times P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,km_{n})}),

Now let
Z^{\prime}_{1},Z^{\prime}_{2},\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Z}
be drawn independently of the data. Then

\displaystyle\biggl|\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm CBM}_{n}}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\mathbb{E}_{{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)}))^{m_{n}}}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\Bigl|\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}\left(Y_{(n,A_{n,k})},Y_{(n,A_{n,k+1})}\right)}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Z}}\right]\!-\!\mathbb{E}_{{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)}))^{m_{n}}}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\sum_{i\in A_{n,k}\cup A_{n,k+1}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right).

Step 3: concentration around the mean of conditional deviance.

By Lemma
D.8
, there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\mathbb{E}_{{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)}))^{m_{n}}}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]-\frac{m_{n}}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\textnormal{Dev}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{k})\right)\biggr|

\displaystyle=\biggl|\frac{2}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\mathbb{E}_{{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)}))^{m_{n}}}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]-\frac{m_{n}}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\textnormal{Dev}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{K_{n}-1}\biggl|\frac{2}{n}\mathbb{E}_{{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)}))^{m_{n}}}}\left[{f_{n}(Y,Y^{\prime})}\right]-\frac{m_{n}}{n}\textnormal{Dev}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right)\biggr|

\displaystyle\hskip 298.75394pt+\frac{m_{n}}{n}\bigl|\textnormal{Dev}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},K_{n})})\right)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq(K_{n}-1)\cdot\frac{m_{n}}{n}\cdot\frac{C}{\sqrt{m_{n}}}+\frac{4m_{n}}{n}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{m_{n}}}+\frac{4m_{n}}{n}.

Moreover, since
1/(K_{n}+1)\leq m_{n}/n\leq 1/K_{n}
, since
Z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{K_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}P_{Z}
, we have by the central limit theorem that

\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{m_{n}}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\textnormal{Dev}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{k})\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{K_{n}}-\frac{m_{n}}{n}\biggr)4K_{n}+\biggl|\frac{1}{K_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\textnormal{Dev}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{k})\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]\biggr|={\rm O}_{P}(K_{n}^{-1/2}).

Combining these bounds with our conclusions from Steps 1 and 2, then,

\left|{\rm CBM}_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left[{\textnormal{Dev}(P_{Y|Z})}\right]\right|\leq\frac{8}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\sum_{i\in A_{n,k}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right)\\
+\frac{8}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\sum_{i\in A_{n,k+1}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right)+{\rm O}_{P}(K_{n}^{-1/2})+{\rm O}(m_{n}^{-1/2}).

Step 4: the Wasserstein distance.

Finally, we bound the remaining terms. First,

\frac{8}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\sum_{i\in A_{n,k}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right)={\rm o}_{P}(1),

by Lemma
D.5
below (applied with sets
B_{n,k}=A_{n,k}
for
k\in[K_{n}-1]
, and
B_{n,K_{n}}=\emptyset
). Similarly,

\frac{8}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}-1}\sum_{i\in A_{n,k+1}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\left(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\right)={\rm o}_{P}(1),

by Lemma
D.5
again (applied with sets
B_{n,k}=A_{n,k+1}
for
k\in[K_{n}-1]
, and
B_{n,K_{n}}=\emptyset
). This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
13

The argument closely follows that of Theorem
2
, given in Section
2.1
. The only modifications are to accommodate the multivariate notions of monotonic functions and stochastic monotonicity introduced in Section
5
.

Step 1: deterministic properties of the
p
-value.

Fix
\alpha\in[0,1]
and define
p:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
by

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})\geq T(\mathbf{x})}\right\}.

Then the
p
-value in (
5
) can be written as
p=p(\mathbf{X})
, and satisfies the deterministic inequality (
8
).
Moreover, by the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
2
,
p(\mathbf{x})
is nonincreasing in each
x_{i_{\ell}}
and nondecreasing in each
x_{j_{\ell}}
, where monotonicity of functions is interpreted coordinatewise as in Section
5
, and
(\psi_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{L}
are anti-monotonic as in (
28
).

Step 2: comparison to the sharp null.

We define the sharp null analogously to the proof of Theorem
2
. Writing
P_{i}=P_{X|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{i})
for
i\in[n]
, set

\bar{P}_{\ell}=\tfrac{1}{2}P_{i_{\ell}}+\tfrac{1}{2}P_{j_{\ell}}

for
\ell\in[L]
. Under Assumption
2
, conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
, for any nondecreasing function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{X\sim P_{X\mid Z_{i_{\ell}}}}}\left[{f(X)}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{{X\sim\bar{P}_{\ell}}}\left[{f(X)}\right],\qquad\ell\in[L],

(65)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{X\sim\bar{P}_{\ell}}}\left[{f(X)}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{{X\sim P_{X\mid Z_{j_{\ell}}}}}\left[{f(X)}\right],\qquad\ell\in[L].

(66)

Further, conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
,

\mathbf{X}\sim P_{1}\times\dots\times P_{n},

and we sample

\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}\sim(P_{\sharp})_{1}\times\dots\times(P_{\sharp})_{n},

where
(P_{\sharp})_{i_{\ell}}=(P_{\sharp})_{j_{\ell}}=\bar{P}_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[L]
, and
(P_{\sharp})_{i}=P_{i}
for indices not in any matched pair. As in the proof of Theorem
2
, for any
\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}
,

(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})^{\mathbf{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{d}}}{{=}}\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}

conditional on
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
.

Combining the monotonicity of
p(\mathbf{x})
from Step 1 with the stochastic ordering relations (
65
) and (
66
) , and using the fact that
t\mapsto{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{t\leq\alpha}\right\}
is nonincreasing, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X})\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{X}\sim\otimes_{i=1}^{n}P_{i}}}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X})\leq\alpha}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbf{X}_{\sharp}\sim\otimes_{i=1}^{n}(P_{\sharp})_{i}}}\left[{{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})\leq\alpha}\right\}}\right]=\mathbb{P}\left\{{p(\mathbf{X}_{\sharp})\leq\alpha}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right\}.

Step 3: validity under the sharp null.

This step is identical to its counterpart in the proof of Theorem
2
.

Appendix D
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma D.1
.

It holds that deterministically, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})\leq\alpha}\right\}\leq\alpha,

where
p(\cdot)
is as defined in (
7
).

Proof.

Consider a bijection
\sigma:[2^{L}]\rightarrow\{-1,1\}^{L}
such that
T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(1)})\geq\ldots\geq T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(2^{L})})
, and let
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,2^{L}\}
be such that
\alpha\in\bigl[r/2^{L},(r+1)/2^{L}\bigr)
. Then, since we have
\sum_{k=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(k)})\geq T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(j)})}\right\}\in[2^{L}]
for each
j\in[2^{L}]
, we have deterministically that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{\pm 1\}^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}})\leq\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{p(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(j)})\leq\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(k)})\geq T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(j)})}\right\}\leq\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\sum_{k=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(k)})\geq T(\mathbf{x}^{\sigma(j)})}\right\}\leq r}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{\sum_{k=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{k\leq j}\right\}\leq r}\right\}=\frac{1}{2^{L}}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{L}}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{j\leq r}\right\}=\frac{r}{2^{L}}\leq\alpha,

as required.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

For any
p,q\in(0,1)
, we have

\mathrm{H}^{2}\bigl(\textnormal{Ber}(p),\textnormal{Ber}(q)\bigr)\leq\frac{2(p-q)^{2}}{(p+q)(2-p-q)}\leq\min\biggl\{\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{p(1-p)},\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{q(1-q)}\biggr\}.

Proof.

For any
p,q\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{2}\bigl(\textnormal{Ber}(p),\textnormal{Ber}(q)\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\sqrt{p}-\sqrt{q})^{2}+(\sqrt{1-p}-\sqrt{1-q})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{(\sqrt{p}+\sqrt{q})^{2}}+\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{(\sqrt{1-p}+\sqrt{1-q})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{p}+\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{(1-p)}=\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{p(1-p)}.

By symmetry, the bound
\frac{2(p-q)^{2}}{(p+q)(2-p-q)}\leq\frac{(p-q)^{2}}{q(1-q)}
also holds.
∎

Lemma D.3
.

For any distribution
P
on
\mathbb{R}
,

2\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right)\leq\textnormal{Dev}(P)\leq 4\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right),

where
\textnormal{Dev}(\cdot)
is the deviance of a distribution, defined in (
46
). Moreover, if
P
is symmetric around its mean, then
\textnormal{Dev}(P)=4\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
F^{-1}
denote the quantile function of
P
(i.e., the generalized inverse of its CDF).
For any
q\in(0,1)
, by monotonicity of
F
, we have

\bigl\{F^{-1}(1-q)-F^{-1}(1/2)\bigr\}\cdot\bigl\{F^{-1}(1/2)-F^{-1}(q)\bigr\}\geq 0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\textnormal{Dev}(P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-F^{-1}(1/2)+F^{-1}(1/2)-F^{-1}(q)\bigr)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-F^{-1}(1/2)\bigr)^{2}+\bigl(F^{-1}(1/2)-F^{-1}(q)\bigr)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=2\,\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{\bigl(F^{-1}(q)-F^{-1}(1/2)\bigr)^{2}}\right]\geq 2\,\mathrm{Var}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left({F^{-1}(q)}\right)=2\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right),

since when
q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)
we have
F^{-1}(q)\sim P
by construction. For the upper bound, writing
\mu=\mathbb{E}_{{X\sim P}}\left[{X}\right]
,

\displaystyle\textnormal{Dev}(P)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-\mu+\mu-F^{-1}(q)\bigr)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{2\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-\mu\bigr)^{2}+2\bigl(F^{-1}(q)-\mu\bigr)^{2}}\right]

(67)

\displaystyle=4\,\mathbb{E}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left[{\bigl(F^{-1}(q)-\mu\bigr)^{2}}\right]=4\,\mathrm{Var}_{{q\sim\textnormal{Unif}(0,1)}}\left({F^{-1}(q)}\right)=4\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right),

Next we turn to the special case that
P
is symmetric around its mean. In this case, symmetry of the distribution around its mean ensures that
F^{-1}(1-q)-\mu=\mu-F^{-1}(q)
holds for almost every
q\in[0,1]
(more precisely, this can fail only at discontinuities of the CDF of
P
). Therefore,

\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-\mu+\mu-F^{-1}(q)\bigr)^{2}=2\bigl(F^{-1}(1-q)-\mu\bigr)^{2}+2\bigl(F^{-1}(q)-\mu\bigr)^{2}

for almost every
q\in[0,1]
, and so the inequality in the step (
67
) above is in fact an equality. This proves
\textnormal{Dev}(P)=4\,\mathrm{Var}_{{X\sim P}}\left({X}\right)
, as desired.
∎

Lemma D.4
.

Suppose
a\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
c=(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
be the concatenation of the two vectors. If
\textnormal{Med}(c)<\textnormal{Med}(a)
, then
\textnormal{Med}(c)\geq\textnormal{Med}(b)
; similarly, if
\textnormal{Med}(c)>\textnormal{Med}(a)
, then
\textnormal{Med}(c)\leq\textnormal{Med}(b)
.

Proof.

We will assume that
\textnormal{Med}(c)<\textnormal{Med}(a)
and
\textnormal{Med}(c)<\textnormal{Med}(b)
, and will show that this leads to a contradiction. (The second claim is proved analogously and so we omit the proof.)

We first calculate

\bigl|\{i\in[m]:a_{i}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|\leq\bigl|\{i\in[m]:a_{i}<\textnormal{Med}(a)\}\bigr|\leq\begin{cases}\frac{m}{2},&\textnormal{ if $m$ is even,}\\
\frac{m-1}{2},&\textnormal{ if $m$ is odd},\end{cases}

where the first step holds since we have assumed
\textnormal{Med}(c)<\textnormal{Med}(a)
, and the second step uses the definition of the median. Similarly,

\bigl|\{j\in[n]:b_{j}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|\leq\bigl|\{j\in[n]:b_{j}<\textnormal{Med}(b)\}\bigr|\leq\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2},&\textnormal{ if $n$ is even,}\\
\frac{n-1}{2},&\textnormal{ if $n$ is odd}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, again by definition of the median,

\bigl|\{i\in[m]:a_{i}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|+\bigl|\{j\in[n]:b_{j}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|\\
=\bigl|\{k\in[m+n]:c_{k}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|\geq\begin{cases}\frac{m+n}{2},&\textnormal{ if $m+n$ is even},\\
\frac{m+n+1}{2},&\textnormal{ if $m+n$ is odd}.\end{cases}

So far, then, we have shown that

\frac{m-{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\textnormal{$m$ is odd}}}}{2}+\frac{n-{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\textnormal{$n$ is odd}}}}{2}\geq\frac{m+n+{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\textnormal{$m+n$ is odd}}}}{2}.

If either
m
or
n
is odd (or both), this immediately yields a contradiction. The remaining case is that
m
and
n
are both even, and

\bigl|\{i\in[m]:a_{i}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|=\bigl|\{i\in[m]:a_{i}<\textnormal{Med}(a)\}\bigr|=\frac{m}{2}

and

\bigl|\{j\in[n]:b_{j}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)\}\bigr|=\bigl|\{j\in[n]:b_{j}<\textnormal{Med}(b)\}\bigr|=\frac{n}{2}.

Letting
a_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq a_{(m)}
denote the order statistics of
a
, and similarly letting
b_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq b_{(n)}
denote the order statistics of
b
, we therefore have

a_{(m/2)}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)<a_{(m/2+1)}\textnormal{ and }b_{(n/2)}\leq\textnormal{Med}(c)<b_{(n/2+1)}.

In particular, writing
c_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq c_{(n+m)}
for the order statistics of
c
, this means that

c_{((m+n)/2)}=\max\big\{a_{(m/2)},b_{(n/2)}\big\}\textnormal{ and }c_{((m+n)/2+1)}=\min\{a_{(m/2+1)},b_{(n/2+1)}\big\}.

By definition of the median, then,

\textnormal{Med}(c)=\frac{c_{((m+n)/2)}+c_{((m+n)/2+1)}}{2}=\frac{\max\big\{a_{(m/2)},b_{(n/2)}\big\}+\min\{a_{(m/2+1)},b_{(n/2+1)}\big\}}{2}\\
\geq\min\left\{\frac{a_{(m/2)}+a_{(m/2+1)}}{2},\frac{b_{(n/2)}+b_{(n/2+1)}}{2}\right\}=\min\left\{\textnormal{Med}(a),\textnormal{Med}(b)\right\}.

This contradicts our assumption, and completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.5
.

Let

P_{(Y,Z)}
be a distribution on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}
, and let
Z_{1},Z_{1}^{\prime},Z_{2},Z_{2}^{\prime},\ldots
be independent draws from the corresponding marginal
P_{Z}
on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
(K_{n})
be a sequence of positive integers with
K_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and for each
n
, let
B_{n,1},\dots,B_{n,K_{n}}\subseteq[n]
be a collection of disjoint sets such that
7
7

7

If
B_{n,k}=\emptyset
for some
k\in[K_{n}]
, then we interpret
\max_{i\in B_{n,k}}\bigl|i/n-k/K_{n}\bigr|=0
.

\max_{k\in[K_{n}]}\max_{i\in B_{n,k}}\left|\frac{i}{n}-\frac{k}{K_{n}}\right|\to 0.

Then, as
n\to\infty

\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\bigl(P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z_{(n,i)}),P_{Y|Z}(\cdot\mid Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr)\Biggr]\to 0.

Remark 1
.

The intuition is that, at each value of
n
, we should have
\frac{i}{n}\approx\frac{k}{K_{n}}
and therefore
Z_{(n,i)}\approx Z^{\prime}_{(K_{n},k)}
, for all
k
and all
i\in B_{n,k}
.

Proof.

Let
F_{Z}^{-1}
be the quantile function corresponding to
P_{Z}
, so that if
U\sim\textnormal{Unif}[0,1]
then
F_{Z}^{-1}(U)\sim P_{Z}
. Now define
\tilde{P}_{Y|U}(\cdot\mid u)=P_{Y|Z}\bigl(\cdot\mid F_{Z}^{-1}(u)\bigr)
, i.e., the conditional distribution of
Y
given
U
where
Z=F_{Z}^{-1}(U)
and we sample
Y
conditional on
Z
from
P_{Y\mid Z}
. Since
F_{Z}^{-1}
is monotone, it suffices to prove that

\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\bigl(\tilde{P}_{Y|U}(\cdot\mid U_{(n,i)}),\tilde{P}_{Y|U}(\cdot\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr)\Biggr]\to 0,

where
U_{1},V_{1},U_{2},V_{2},\dots
are independent and identically distributed Unif
[0,1]
random variables.
Writing
F_{Y|U}
for the distribution function corresponding to the conditional distribution
\tilde{P}_{Y|U}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}{\rm d}_{\rm W}\bigl(\tilde{P}_{Y|U}(\cdot\mid U_{(n,i)}),\tilde{P}_{Y|U}(\cdot\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt=\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}\int_{-1}^{1}\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{(n,i)})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr|\;\mathsf{d}t\Biggr]

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt=\int_{-1}^{1}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{(n,i)})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr|\Biggr]\;\mathsf{d}t,

(68)

where the last step follows by Fubini’s theorem.

Now, we define some additional notation: for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in[n]
, let

V_{n,j}=\begin{cases}V_{(K_{n},k)}&\text{if }U_{j}=U_{(n,i)}\penalty\ \text{for some }i\in B_{n,k},\\
U_{j}&\text{if }U_{j}=U_{(n,i)}\penalty\ \text{for some }i\in[n]\setminus(B_{n,1}\cup\dots\cup B_{n,K_{n}}).\end{cases}

(69)

Then for every
t\in[0,1]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{(n,i)})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{j})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{n,j})|.

Moreover,
|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{j})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{n,j})|
is identically distributed for each
j=1,\dots,n
(when
n
is fixed). Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{(n,i)})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr|\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{j})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{n,j})\bigr|}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{1})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{n,1})\bigr|\bigr].

By Lemma
D.6
below,

\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{1})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{n,1})\bigr|=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1).

Since
F_{Y\mid U}(t\mid\cdot)
is a bounded function, we deduce that

\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\sum_{i\in B_{n,k}}\bigl|F_{Y|U}(t\mid U_{(n,i)})-F_{Y|U}(t\mid V_{(K_{n},k)})\bigr|\Biggr]\to 0

for every
t\in[-1,1]
. The result now follows by (
68
) and the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Lemma D.6
.

Let
U_{1},V_{1},U_{2},V_{2},\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textnormal{Unif}[0,1]
, and let
(K_{n})
be a sequence of positive integers with
K_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Furthermore, for each
n
, let
B_{n,1},\dots,B_{n,K_{n}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
be a collection of disjoint sets such that

\max_{k\in[K_{n}]}\max_{i\in B_{n,k}}\biggl|\frac{i}{n}-\frac{k}{K_{n}}\biggr|\to 0.

Then, for any Borel measurable
g:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, we have
|g(U_{1})-g(V_{n,1})|=\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)
,
where
V_{n,1}
is defined as in (
69
).

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
. For any Borel measurable
h:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{|g(U_{1})-g(V_{n,1})|>\epsilon}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{|h(U_{1})-h(V_{n,1})|>\epsilon}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(U_{1})\neq h(U_{1})}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(V_{n,1})\neq h(V_{n,1})}\right\}.

(70)

By Lusin’s theorem (restated for convenience in Theorem
D.9
), we can find a continuous function
h:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathbb{P}_{{U\sim{\rm Unif}[0,1]}}\left\{{g(U)\neq h(U)}\right\}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

Next, for each
k=1,\dots,K_{n}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{n,k}
denote the event that
U_{1}=U_{(n,i)}
for some
i\in B_{n,k}
, which has probability
\frac{|B_{n,k}|}{n}
. Further, define
\mathcal{E}_{n,*}
to be the event that
U_{1}=U_{(n,i)}
for some
i\in[n]\setminus(B_{n,1}\cup\dots\cup B_{n,K_{n}})
. By construction of
V_{n,1}
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{n,k}
we have
V_{n,1}=V_{(K_{n},k)}
, and on
\mathcal{E}_{n,*}
we have
V_{n,1}=U_{1}
.
Therefore, letting
\pi_{n}
denote a permutation of
[K_{n}]
such that
V_{(K_{n},k)}=V_{\pi_{n}(k)}
for
k\in[K_{n}]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(V_{n,1})\neq h(V_{n,1})}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathcal{E}_{n,k},\ g(V_{(K_{n},k)})\neq h(V_{(K_{n},k)}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathcal{E}_{n,*},g(U_{1})\neq h(U_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(*)}}{{=}}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathcal{E}_{n,k}}\right\}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(V_{(K_{n},k)})\neq h(V_{(K_{n},k)}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{\mathcal{E}_{n,*},g(U_{1})\neq h(U_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\frac{|B_{n,k}|}{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(V_{(K_{n},k)})\neq h(V_{(K_{n},k)}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(U_{1})\neq h(U_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\frac{|B_{n,k}|}{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(V_{\pi_{n}(k)})\neq h(V_{\pi_{n}(k)})}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(U_{1})\neq h(U_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\frac{|B_{n,\pi_{n}^{-1}(k)}|}{n}\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(V_{k})\neq h(V_{k})}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{{g(U_{1})\neq h(U_{1})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\frac{|B_{n,k}|}{n}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{4}+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},

where the step marked
\rm(*)
holds because
\mathcal{E}_{n,k}
depends only on
(U_{1},\dots,U_{n})
, while
V_{(K_{n},k)}
depends only on
(V_{1},\dots,V_{K_{n}})
, and therefore
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{n,k}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}V_{(K_{n},k)}
, while the penultimate step holds since
U_{1}\sim\textnormal{Unif}[0,1]
, and
V_{k}\sim\textnormal{Unif}[0,1]
for each
k
.
Finally, let
\widehat{F}_{U,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{U_{i}}
and
\widehat{F}_{V,K_{n}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{K_{n}}\delta_{V_{i}}
be the empirical distribution of
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
and
V_{1},\ldots,V_{K_{n}}
respectively. We may assume that
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
are distinct, since this holds almost surely, so if
i\in[n]
is such that
U_{1}=U_{(n,i)}
, then we have

\widehat{F}_{U,n}(U_{1})=\frac{i}{n}.

Let
F_{\textnormal{Unif}}
denote the distribution function of the
\textnormal{Unif}[0,1]
distribution, i.e.,
F_{\textnormal{Unif}}(t)=t
for
t\in[0,1]
. If
i
further satisfies
i\in B_{n,k}
for some
k\in[K_{n}]
, then
V_{n,1}=V_{(K_{n},k)}
, so

\displaystyle|U_{1}-V_{n,1}|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl|U_{1}-\frac{i}{n}\biggr|+\biggl|V_{n,1}-\frac{k}{K_{n}}\biggr|+\biggl|\frac{i}{n}-\frac{k}{K_{n}}\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\widehat{F}_{U,n}-F_{\textnormal{Unif}}\bigr\|_{\infty}+\bigl\|\widehat{F}_{V,K_{n}}-F_{\textnormal{Unif}}\bigr\|_{\infty}+\max_{k\in[K_{n}]}\max_{i\in B_{n,k}}\biggl|\frac{i}{n}-\frac{k}{K_{n}}\biggr|.

If instead we have
i\in[n]\setminus(B_{n,1}\cup\dots\cup B_{n,K_{n}})
, then this bound holds trivially, since in that case
V_{n,1}=U_{1}
by definition. Since the first two terms are each
\mathrm{o}_{P}(1)
by the Glivenko–Cantelli theorem, while the last term
\max_{k\in[K_{n}]}\max_{i\in B_{n,k}}\biggl|\frac{i}{n}-\frac{k}{K_{n}}\biggr|
is vanishing by assumption, it follows that
V_{n,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}U_{1}
, so by the continuous mapping theorem,
\mathbb{P}\left\{{|h(U_{1})-h(V_{n,1})|>\epsilon}\right\}\leq\epsilon/4
for sufficiently large
n
. The result therefore follows from (
70
).
∎

Lemma D.7
.

The function
f_{n}
from (
63
) satisfies the Lipschitz property

\bigl|f_{n}(y,y^{\prime})-f_{n}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\bigr|\leq 4\|y-\tilde{y}\|_{1}+4\|y^{\prime}-\tilde{y}^{\prime}\|_{1}

for
(y,y^{\prime}),(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\in[-1,1]^{m_{n}}\times[-1,1]^{m_{n}}
.

Proof of Lemma
D.7
.

Since
t\mapsto t^{2}_{+}
is
4
-Lipschitz on
[-2,2]
, we have for each
i=1,\ldots,\lfloor m/2\rfloor
that

\left|(y_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-y^{\prime}_{(i)})^{2}_{+}-(\tilde{y}_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{(i)})^{2}_{+}\right|\leq 4|y_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-\tilde{y}_{(m_{n}+1-i)}|+4|y^{\prime}_{(i)}-\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{(i)}|.

Therefore,

\left|f_{n}(y,y^{\prime})-f_{n}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\right|\leq 4\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}|y_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-\tilde{y}_{(m_{n}+1-i)}|+4\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}|y^{\prime}_{(i)}-\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{(i)}|.

Writing
y_{()}=(y_{(1)},\dots,y_{(m_{n})})
for the sorted vector of
y
, and same for
y^{\prime}_{()}
,
\tilde{y}_{()}
, and
\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{()}
, we deduce that

\left|f_{n}(y,y^{\prime})-f_{n}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\right|\leq 4\|y_{()}-\tilde{y}_{()}\|_{1}+4\|y^{\prime}_{()}-\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{()}\|_{1}.

Finally, we write
\hat{P}_{y}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\delta_{y_{i}}
and
\hat{P}_{\tilde{y}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\delta_{\tilde{y}_{i}}
. Then, from the definition of the
L_{1}
-Wasserstein distance
{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\cdot,\cdot)
, it holds that

\|y_{()}-\tilde{y}_{()}\|_{1}={\rm d}_{\rm W}(\hat{P}_{y},\hat{P}_{\tilde{y}})\leq\|y-\tilde{y}\|_{1},

and a similar inequality also holds for
y^{\prime},\tilde{y}^{\prime}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.8
.

Let
Q
be any distribution on
[-1,1]
, and let
A=(A_{1},\dots,A_{m_{n}})\sim Q^{m_{n}}
and
B=(B_{1},\dots,B_{m_{n}})\sim Q^{m_{n}}
. Then, with
f_{n}
as defined in (
63
), there exists a univeral constant
C>0
such that

\biggl|\frac{2}{m_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,B)}\right]-\textnormal{Dev}(Q)\biggr|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{m_{n}}}.

Proof of Lemma
D.8
.

First let
A_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq A_{(m_{n})}
and
B_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq B_{(m_{n})}
be the order statistics of
A
and
B
, and write
A_{()}=(A_{(1)},\dots,A_{(m_{n})})
and
B_{()}=(B_{(1)},\dots,B_{(m_{n})})
for the corresponding sorted vectors. Then

f_{n}(A,B)=f_{n}(A_{()},B_{()})

since
(y,y^{\prime})\mapsto f_{n}(y,y^{\prime})
is invariant to permutations of
y
, and invariant to permutations of
y^{\prime}
.

Next, let
F_{Q}^{-1}
denote the quantile function corresponding to
Q
. Let
U_{1},\dots,U_{m_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\textnormal{Unif}[0,1]
, so we can assume without loss of generality that
A_{i}=F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{i})
for
i\in[m_{n}]
. Define
A^{\prime}=(A_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,A_{m_{n}}^{\prime})
by
A^{\prime}_{i}=F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{i})
for
i\in[m_{n}]
, and let
A^{\prime}_{()}
denote the corresponding sorted vector. Then by Lemma
D.7
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,B)}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})}\right]\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|f_{n}(A,B)-f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})\right|}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left|f_{n}(A_{()},B_{()})-f_{n}(A_{()},A^{\prime}_{()})\right|}\right]\leq 4\mathbb{E}\left[{\|A^{\prime}_{()}-B_{()}\|_{1}}\right].

(71)

Next, let
U_{(1)}\leq\dots\leq U_{(m_{n})}
be the order statistics of
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m_{n}}
. Then
A_{(i)}=F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{(i)})
and
A^{\prime}_{(i)}=F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{(m_{n}+1-i)})
for each
i\in[m_{n}]
. Now, for
i\in[m_{n}]
, define the event

\mathcal{E}_{i}:=\begin{cases}\{U_{(i)}<1/2\}&\text{if }i\leq\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor,\\
\emptyset&\text{if }i=\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil\,\text{when }m_{n}/2\notin\mathbb{N},\\
\{U_{(i)}\geq 1/2\}&\text{if }i\geq\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil+1.\end{cases}

Thus, it follows that
