\displaystyle f_{n}(A^{\prime},A)=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}(A^{\prime}_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-A_{(i)})^{2}_{+}=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{(i)})-F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{(i)})\bigr\}^{2}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}}}

and

\displaystyle f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}(A_{(m_{n}+1-i)}-A^{\prime}_{(i)})^{2}_{+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{(m_{n}+1-i)})-F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{(m_{n}+1-i)})\bigr\}^{2}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{m_{n}+1-i}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil+1}^{m_{n}}\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{(i)})-F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{(i)})\bigr\}^{2}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}}}.

Moreover, since
(A,A^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(A^{\prime},A)
, we have
f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}f_{n}(A^{\prime},A)
.
This further implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{f_{n}(A^{\prime},A)+f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})}{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{(i)})-F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{(i)})\bigr\}^{2}\cdot{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}}}\biggr].

By definition,
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{i})-F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]=\textnormal{Dev}(Q)
for each
i\in[m_{n}]
. Hence,

\displaystyle\biggl|\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{m_{n}}{2}\textnormal{Dev}(Q)\biggr|

\displaystyle=\biggl|\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{i})-F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\biggr]\biggr|

\displaystyle=\biggl|\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,A^{\prime})}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\bigl\{F_{Q}^{-1}(1-U_{(i)})-F_{Q}^{-1}(U_{(i)})\bigr\}^{2}\biggr]\biggr|\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}\biggr],

(72)

where the last step follows by recalling that
Q
is supported on
[-1,1]
.
Next, we define
I_{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\{U_{i}<1/2\}}}
.
Then by definition of the events
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{U_{(i)}\geq 1/2}}=\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor-\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{U_{(i)}<1/2}}=\max\{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor-I_{1},0\},

and similarly

\sum_{i=\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil+1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}=\sum_{i=\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil+1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{U_{(i)}<1/2}}=\max\left\{I_{1}-\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil,0\right\}.

Therefore,

\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}\leq 1+\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}+\sum_{i=\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil+1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}\\
=1+\max\{\lfloor m_{n}/2\rfloor-I_{1},0\}+\max\left\{I_{1}-\lceil m_{n}/2\rceil,0\right\}\leq 1+\left|I_{1}-\frac{m_{n}}{2}\right|.

Since
I_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\{U_{i}<1/2\}}}\sim\textnormal{Bin}(m_{n},1/2)
and
m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we further have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}_{i}^{c}}}\biggr]\leq 1+\mathbb{E}\left[{\biggl|I_{1}-\frac{m_{n}}{2}\biggr|}\right]\leq 1+\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left({I_{1}}\right)}=1+\frac{\sqrt{m_{n}}}{2}\leq\frac{3\sqrt{m_{n}}}{2}.

(73)

Next we turn to bounding
\|A^{\prime}_{()}-B_{()}\|_{1}
.
Write
\widehat{P}_{A^{\prime}}
and
\widehat{P}_{B}
for the empirical distributions of
A^{\prime}
and
B
, respectively. Then

\|A^{\prime}_{()}-B_{()}\|_{1}=m_{n}{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\widehat{P}_{A^{\prime}},\widehat{P}_{B})\leq m_{n}{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\widehat{P}_{A^{\prime}},Q)+m_{n}{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\widehat{P}_{B},Q).

By bounds on the empirical
1
-Wasserstein distance
(
Lei, 2020
, Theorem 3.1)
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm d}_{\rm W}(\widehat{P}_{A^{\prime}},Q)}\right]\leq C^{\prime}m_{n}^{-1/2}

for a universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
, and same for
\widehat{P}_{B}
. We conclude from this together with (
71
), (
72
) and (
73
) that

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}\left[{f_{n}(A,B)}\right]-\frac{m_{n}}{2}\textnormal{Dev}(Q)\Bigr|\leq 8C^{\prime}\sqrt{m_{n}}+3\sqrt{m_{n}},

as required.
∎

Lemma D.9

(A version of Lusin’s theorem)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function and let
\nu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Then, given any
\epsilon>0
, there is a compactly supported continuous function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\nu\{x:f(x)\neq g(x)\}<\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof follows by combining Theorem 2.18 and Theorem 2.24 of
Rudin (1987)
.
∎

Appendix E
Additional experiments

In this section, we evaluate the proposed
PairSwap-ICI
testing procedure alongside several well-established benchmarks from the literature in the following simulation settings. Let
Y_{1},Z_{1},\ldots,Y_{n},Z_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Writing
\mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
and
\mathbf{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
, we assume that
\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\mid(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
follows one of the following models:

(1)

\mathbf{X}=\beta_{n}\,\mathbf{Y}+\mathbf{Z}+{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
,

(2)

\mathbf{X}=\beta_{n}\,\mathbf{Y}^{2}+\mathbf{Z}+{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
,

(3)

\mathbf{X}=\beta_{n}\,\mathbf{Y}^{2}+\mathbf{Z}+{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
,

(4)

\mathbf{X}=\beta_{n}\,\mathbf{Y}+\mathbf{Z}+0.4\cdot\sin(2\pi\mathbf{Z})+{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
,

where
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}t_{4}
are generated independently of
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
, and we write
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})
and
{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n})
. In all cases,
\beta_{n}=0
corresponds to the isotonic CI null
H_{0}^{\mathrm{ICI}}
, while
\beta_{n}>0
represents a deviation from the null.

We consider the following tests of conditional independence:

•

The
PairSwap-ICI
test with the plug-in matching
\hat{M}
and the weight matrix
\hat{W}
, as defined in (
16
). With the linear kernel
\psi(x,x^{\prime})=x-x^{\prime}
, the weight matrix
\hat{W}=(\hat{W}_{ij})_{i,j\in[n]}
is computed using estimates
\hat{E}_{ij}
of
\mathbb{E}\left[{\psi(X_{i},X_{j})}\ \middle|\ {\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}}\right]
, constructed via sample splitting.

•

The
Generalized Covariance Measure
(GCM) test, introduced by
Shah and Peters (2020)
. Define

R(X,Y,Z):=\bigl(X-\hat{\mathbb{E}}(X\mid Z)\bigr)\bigl(Y-\hat{\mathbb{E}}(Y\mid Z)\bigr),

where
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(X\mid Z)
and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(Y\mid Z)
are estimates of the corresponding conditional means obtained via sample splitting. We then compute the
p
-value

p_{\rm GCM}=2-2\cdot\Phi\!\Biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})\bigr|}{\bigl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R^{2}(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})-\bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}^{1/2}}\Biggr).

•

The
Projected Covariance Measure
(PCM) test, introduced by
Lundborg et al. (2024)
. At a high level, PCM follows a structure similar to GCM, but operates on

L(X,Y,Z)=\bigl\{f(X,Z)-\hat{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(f(X,Z)\mid Z\bigr)\bigr\}\bigl(Y-\hat{\mathbb{E}}(Y\mid Z)\bigr),

where
f
is a data-adaptive function selected via sample splitting to capture departures from conditional mean independence. As in GCM, the
p
-value is computed as

p_{\rm PCM}=2-2\cdot\Phi\!\Biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})\bigr|}{\bigl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{2}(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})-\bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}^{1/2}}\Biggr).

•

The
Conditional Randomization Test
(CRT), proposed by
Candès et al. (2018)
. Given a test statistic

T:\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n}\times\mathcal{Z}^{n})\to\mathbb{R}

and a sampler
\hat{P}_{X\mid Z}
approximating the true
P_{X\mid Z}
, we draw

\mathbf{X}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{X}^{(M)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{iid}}}{{\sim}}\hat{P}_{X\mid Z}
and compute the
p
-value

p_{\rm CRT}=\frac{1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{M}{\mathbbm{1}}\left\{{T(\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})\geq T(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})}\right\}}{M+1}.

In our experiments, we take
T(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}
, and use isotonic distributional regression
(
Henzi et al., 2021
)
to estimate
\hat{P}_{X\mid Z}
in order to preserve stochastic monotonicity.

Type I error control.

We evaluate the Type I error as the empirical rejection probability when
\beta_{n}=0
, averaged over
500
Monte Carlo repetitions for each method. Figure
6
displays the Type I error curves for each method as a function of the sample size. Consistent with Theorem
2
,
PairSwap-ICI
maintains finite-sample Type I error control across all sample sizes. Although Model (4) exhibits mild violations of Assumption
1
and thus falls outside the scope of Theorem
2
, the empirical results indicate that the proposed
PairSwap-ICI
framework continues to perform reliably in practice. This suggests a degree of robustness to such deviations and its broader applicability beyond such setting. By contrast, PCM and CRT provide only asymptotic validity guarantees, and we observe noticeable deviations from the nominal level
\alpha
in finite samples. GCM is also asymptotically valid; however, in these simulation settings, its Type I error also remains below the nominal level across the sample sizes considered.

Figure 6:
Type I error as a function of sample size under the simulation setting of Appendix
E
. The nominal level
\alpha=0.1
is indicated by the dotted black line.

Power.

Figure 7:
Power as a function of sample size under the simulation models of Appendix
E
, with
\beta_{n}=3\,n^{-0.3}
. Columns correspond to different data-generating models.

We next examine the power of the four methods under the alternatives described above, with
\beta_{n}=3\,n^{-0.3}
. Figure
7
reports the power, evaluated as empirical rejection probability at different sample sizes, averaged over
500
independent repetitions.

Model (1) corresponds to the linear Gaussian setting analyzed in Section
4.4
. By Theorem
11
and
12
, when
\beta_{n}\gg n^{-1/2}
, the power of
PairSwap-ICI
converges to
1
, as confirmed by the left panel of Figure
7
. Although competing methods attain power close to
1
at smaller sample sizes in this model, it is important to note several aspects: first, the CRT method does not control Type I error at these smaller sizes (see the left panel of Figure
6
); second, in model (1),
\beta_{n}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Cov}(X,Y\mid Z)}\right]
, which is the target of the GCM test statistic. In this sense, the GCM method is tailored to this setting. The drawback of testing the narrower null hypothesis
H_{0}^{\mathrm{GCM}}:\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{Cov}(X,Y\mid Z)}\right]=0
is illustrated in models (2) and (3), where
H_{0}^{\mathrm{GCM}}
continues to hold despite the fact that conditional independence is violated. Thus, the GCM has no power in these models, as can be seen in the middle two panels of Figure
7
. The CRT suffers a similar drawback, while the PCM has strong power in both models (2) and (3), since the departures from conditional independence also represent violations of conditional mean independence, and are therefore favorable settings for the PCM. Nevertheless, the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure has competitive power with the PCM in both models (2) and (3). Lastly, model (4) closely mimics model (1), and therefore the performances of all the methods are similar under models (1) and (4).

In summary, the
PairSwap-ICI
procedure is the only one of the four methods studied that both retains finite-sample Type I error control in all examples where the stochastic monotonicity holds true, and has competitive power in all settings considered.
